A spanning tree model For chromatic Homology
Aninda Banerjee, Apratim Chakraborty, Swarup Kumar Das, Pravakar Paul

Abstract
. After the discovery of Khovanov homology, which categorifies the Jones polynomial, an analogous categorification of the chromatic polynomial, known as chromatic homology, was introduced. Its graded Euler characteristic recovers the chromatic polynomial. In this paper, we present a spanning tree model for the chromatic complex, i.e., we describe a chain complex generated by certain spanning trees of the graph that is chain homotopy equivalent to the chromatic complex. We employ the spanning tree model over 𝒜 m := ℤ ⁡ [ x ] < x m > \mathcal{A}_{m}:=\frac{\mathbb{Z}[x]}{<x^{m}>} algebra to answer two open questions. First, we establish the conjecture posed by Sazdanovic and Scofield regarding the homological span of chromatic homology over 𝒜 m \mathcal{A}_{m} algebra, demonstrating that for any graph G G with v v vertices and b b blocks, the homological span is v − b v-b . Additionally, we prove a conjecture of Helme-Guizon, Przytycki, and Rong concerning the existence of torsion of order dividing m m in chromatic homology over 𝒜 m \mathcal{A}_{m} algebra.



1.
Introduction

It is a fundamental problem in graph theory to determine the number of ways a graph can be colored using
\lambda
many colors. The chromatic polynomial
P(G,\lambda)
counts the number of such colorings when evaluated at
\lambda
. It was first defined in 1912 by George David Birkhoff in an attempt to solve the four-color theorem for planar graphs, proving that
P(G,4)>0
for all planar graphs would have implied the result.

Let
G
be a connected simple graph with
n
vertices, and fix an ordering on its edges. A spanning tree
T
of
G
is called an
NBC spanning tree
if, for every edge
e\in E(G)\setminus E(T)
,
e
is not the smallest ordered edge in the unique cycle of the subgraph
T\cup\{e\}
of
G
.

There are several expansions of the chromatic polynomial in terms of different graph-theoretic objects. One such expansion expresses the chromatic polynomial in terms of NBC spanning trees. This formulation enables a purely algebraic computation of the chromatic polynomial.

Helme-Guizon-Rong
[
HGR05
]
introduced a Khovanov-type bi-graded homology theory which categorifies the chromatic polynomial. Throughout the paper the chromatic complex will be denoted by
{C_{*,*}(G)}
for a given graph
G
. The chromatic complex of
G
is generated by the enhanced spanning subgraphs of
G
. It should be noted that, although the original definition of chromatic homology was given for the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2}:=\frac{\mathbb{Z}[x]}{<x^{2}>}
, it can be generalized to any graded commutative algebra. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, it will always be assumed that we are working over
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. However, in Section 4, we will explore chromatic homology over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebras where
\mathcal{A}_{m}:=\frac{\mathbb{Z}[x]}{<x^{m}>}
.

We introduce a bi-graded chain complex
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
generated by signed NBC spanning trees whose differential can be easily combinatorially computed from the graph. We will call it spanning tree complex. Its homology will be denoted by
{HST_{*,*}(G)}
. Our main theorem states that the spanning tree complex is chain homotopy equivalent to chromatic complex.

Theorem 1.1
.

The spanning tree complex
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
and the chromatic complex
{C_{*,*}(G)}
are chain homotopic and therefore,
{HST_{i,j}(G)}\cong{H_{i,j}(G)}
.

The graded Euler characteristic
\chi_{q}({CST_{*,*}(G)})
is the alternating sum of the graded dimensions of its cohomology groups, i.e.
\chi_{q}({CST_{*,*}(G)})=\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{i}\cdot q^{j}\operatorname{dim}({HST_{i,j}(G)})
. As a corollary of Theorem
1.1
, we obtain the spanning tree expansion of the chromatic polynomial.

Corollary 1.2
.

The graded Euler characteristic of
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
coincides with the chromatic polynomial of the graph
G
when evaluated at
\lambda=1+q
. Hence we obtain,

P(G;\lambda)=(-1)^{n-1}\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}(1-\lambda)^{i}

where
t_{i}
is the number of NBC spanning trees of
G
which have internal activity
i
(with respect to any fixed ordering of
E(G)
).

Theorem
1.1
can be regarded as a categorification of the spanning tree expansion formula of the chromatic polynomial.

Our theorem builds on the work of Chandler and Sazdanovic
[
CS19
]
. Using methods of algebraic Morse theory, they proved that the chromatic complex (over any commutative graded algebra) deformation retracts to a complex generated by enhanced NBC subgraphs. This can be viewed as a categorification of Whitney’s Broken circuit Theorem.

In a similar vein, using methods from algebraic Morse theory, we prove that chromatic complex is chain homotopy equivalent to a a chain complex generated by the signed NBC spanning trees of the graph. This results in a significant reduction in the number of generators. To emphasize this point, we provide some computations of chromatic homology using the spanning tree model without computer assistance. Moreover, the spanning tree model perspective enables us to explore the chromatic complex using graph-theoretic properties.

The spanning tree model can be extended over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra. We prove the following conjecture on the homological span of chromatic homology over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra posed by Sazdanovic and Scofield
[
SS18
]
.

Conjecture 1.3

(Conjecture 43)
.

[
SS18
]

The homological span of chromatic homology over algebra
\mathcal{A}_{m}
of any graph
G
with
v
vertices and
b
blocks is equal to
hspan\left({{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}\right)=v-b
.

We also prove the following conjecture posed by Helme-Guizon, Przytycki, and Rong on the existence of torsion of order dividing
m
in chromatic homology over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra.

Conjecture 1.4

(Conjecture 25)
.

[
LHG06
]

The cohomology
{{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
of a graph
G
contains a torsion part if and only if
G
has no loops and contains a cycle of order greater than or equal to
3
. In this case,
{{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
has a torsion of order dividing
m
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we review some background material. In Section 3, we introduce the spanning tree model for chromatic homology in the context of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
algebras. We describe the exact combinatorial formula of the differential and discuss some applications and computations. Finally, in Section 4, we generalize the spanning tree model for chromatic homology over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebras and provide proofs of Conjecture
1.3
and Conjecture
1.4
.

2.
Background

2.1.
Algebraic Morse Theory

Morse theory for regular CW-complexes was originally defined by Foreman. An algebraic version of this was introduced by Jöllenbeck and Welker in
[
JW09
]
and independently by Sköldberg in
[
Skö05
]
for chain complexes of arbitrary free
\mathcal{R}
-modules.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a ring with unity, and
C_{\star}=(C_{i},\partial_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a co-chain complex of free
\mathcal{R}
-modules
C_{i}
for all
i
. For two elements
c,c^{\prime}
of homological grading
i
and
i+1
, the coefficient of
c^{\prime}
in the differential of
c
is denoted by
[c,c^{\prime}]
.

Let
\Omega_{i}
be a basis for
C^{i}
such that

C_{i}\cong\bigoplus_{c\in\Omega_{i}}\mathcal{R}c

Let
\Omega=\bigcup_{n}\Omega_{n}
be a basis for the co-chain complex
C^{*}
. The differentials
\partial^{i}
are given by:

(1)

\partial_{i}:\begin{cases}C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}\\
c\rightarrow\partial_{i}(c)=\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\Omega_{i+1}}[c:c^{\prime}].c^{\prime}\end{cases}

Given a co-chain complex
C_{\ast}
, one can construct a directed, weighted graph
G_{C_{\star}}=(V,E)
such that the vertex set of
G
is the basis elements
V=\Omega
, and draw a directed edge
c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
whenever
[c,c^{\prime}]\neq 0
. The weight on each such edge
c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
is defined to be
[c:c^{\prime}]
.

A matching
\mathcal{M}
on a directed graph
G=(V,E)
is a collection of its edges such that no two edges are incident on a common vertex. We can construct another graph
G^{\mathcal{M}}
given such a directed graph
G
with vertex set
V
and the same edge set but just reversing the direction of all arrows in
\mathcal{M}
. Given a co-chain complex
C_{\star}
, we say that a matching
\mathcal{M}
on
G_{C_{\star}}
is acyclic if there are no directed cycles on the graph
G^{\mathcal{M}}_{C_{\star}}
and weights of each matching edge is invertible.

Given a matching
\mathcal{M}
on the graph
G(C_{\star})=(V,E)
, we shall use the following notations:

(1)

A vertex
v\in V
is called critical with respect to
\mathcal{M}
if
v
does not lie in an edge
e\in\mathcal{M}
. We use the notation

\Omega_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}:=\{c\in\Omega_{i}\mid c\text{ is critical}\}

for the set of all critical vertices in the
i
-th homological degree. Let us denote the collection of all possible critical states by
\mathcal{M}^{c}=\cup_{i}\Omega_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

(2)

\mathcal{P}(c,c^{\prime})
is the set of all directed alternating paths from
c
to
c^{\prime}
in the graph
G_{C_{\star}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

(3)

The weight of a path
w(p)
of a path
p=c_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow c_{r}
is given by

w(p):=\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}w(c_{i}\rightarrow c_{i+1})

(4)

w(c\rightarrow c^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{[c:c^{\prime}]},&\text{ if the edge }(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M},\\
[c:c^{\prime}],&\text{if the edge }(c,c^{\prime})\in E-\mathcal{M}.\end{cases}

(5)

\Gamma(c,c^{\prime})=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}(c,c^{\prime})}w(p)
denotes the sum of the weights of all alternating paths from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.1

(Morse Complex)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}_{\star},\partial_{\star},\Omega)
be a free co-chain complex with an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}
. The Morse complex
C_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}}=(C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}},\partial_{i}^{\mathcal{M}})
, with respect to
\mathcal{M}
, is defined by the
\mathcal{R}
-modules
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}:=\bigoplus_{c\in\Omega_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}}\mathcal{R}c
,

\partial_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}:\begin{cases}C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow C_{i+1}^{\mathcal{M}}\\
c\mapsto\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\Omega_{i+1}^{\mathcal{M}}}\Gamma(c,c^{\prime})c^{\prime},\end{cases}

Associated to a given freely generated chain complex
(C_{\star},\partial_{\star})
and an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}
on its Hasse diagram, we have the following maps:

(2)

\displaystyle f:C_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow C_{\star}

\displaystyle f_{i}:C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow C_{i},

\displaystyle f_{i}(c):=\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\Omega_{i}}\Gamma(c,c^{\prime}).c^{\prime},

(3)

\displaystyle g:C_{\star}\rightarrow C_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle g_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}},

\displaystyle g_{i}(c):=\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\Omega_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}}\Gamma(c,c^{\prime}).c^{\prime},

(4)

\displaystyle\chi:C_{\star}\rightarrow C_{\star}

\displaystyle\chi_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1},

\displaystyle\chi_{i}(c):=\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\Omega_{i+1}}\Gamma(c,c^{\prime}).c^{\prime},

Lemma 2.2
.

For the maps defined above, we have the following:

(1)

f
and
g
are chain maps.

(2)

f
and
g
define a chain homotopy. In particular, we have

(a)

f_{i}\circ g_{i}-id_{C_{i}}=\partial\circ\chi_{i+1}+\chi_{i}\circ\partial
,

(b)

g_{i}\circ f_{i}-id_{C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}}=0
.

As a consequence of the above lemma, we obtain the main result in algebraic discrete Morse theory due to the following theorem:

Theorem 2.3

(
[
JW09
]
)
.

Let
(C_{\star},\partial_{\star})
be a freely generated chain complex and
\mathcal{M}
be an acyclic matching on
G_{C_{\star}}
. Then, we the following homotopy equivalence of chain complexes:

(C_{\star},\partial_{\star})\backsimeq\left(C_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}},\partial_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}}\right)

Moreover, for all
i\geq 0
, we have the isomorphism on the (co)-homology level,

H_{i}(C_{\star})\cong H_{i}(C_{\star}^{\mathcal{M}})

2.2.
Shellable Complex and Posets

Definition 2.4
.

An abstract simplicial complex
\Delta(V)
on a vertex set
V
, is a non empty collection of subsets of
V
, such that:

(1)

\{v\}\in\Delta,\forall v\in V
.

(2)

If
F\subseteq G
and
G\in\Delta\implies F\in\Delta
.

A subcomplex
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\Delta
is a subset of
\Delta
and it is itself an abstract simplicial complex. The elements of
\Delta
are called faces. A face that is not properly contained in any other face is called a facet of
\Delta
. A dimension of a face is one less than its cardinality. That is,
dimF=\lvert F\rvert-1.
An abstract simplicial complex
\Delta
is said to be
pure
if all its facets are equicardinal.

Example 2.5
.

Let
V=\{1,2,3\}
. Then
\Delta(V)=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{1,2\},\{2,3\}\{1,3\},\{1,2,3\},\phi\}
defines a simplicial complex.
\Delta^{\prime}=\{\{1\},\{1,2\},\phi\}
is a subcomplex of
\Delta(V)
.

Definition 2.6
.

A shelling of a simplicial complex
\Delta
is a linear order of the facets of
\Delta
,
\{F_{1},F_{2},\cdots F_{p}\}
such that for each pair
F_{i},F_{j}
of facets such that
1\leq i<j\leq p
, there is a facet
F_{k}
satisfying
1\leq k<j
and an element
x\in F_{j}
such that
F_{i}\cap F_{j}\subseteq F_{k}\cap F_{j}=F_{j}-\{x\}
. The linear order is called the shelling order. A simplicial complex is said to be shellable if it is pure and it has a shelling order. The dimension of a simplicial complex is defined as the dimension of the maximum facet. So in the case of pure simplicial complexes, its dimension is the dimension of a facet.

Let us fix some notations for the following section. Let
\Delta
be a
r
dimensional shellable complex with maximal facets
F_{1},F_{2},\cdots F_{k}
listed in a shelling order. Let
\Delta_{i}
is the subcomplex of
\Delta
generated by the first
i
facets for
i=1,2,\cdots t
, i.e.
\Delta_{i}=\{G|G\subset F_{k}\text{ for }k\leq i\}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}(F_{i})=\{x\in F_{i}:F_{i}-x\in\Delta_{i-1}\}
, called the restrictions of
F_{i}
induced by the shelling. Consider
\Delta
to be partially ordered by the set inclusion. Then we denote an interval
[G_{1},G_{2}]:=\{G\in\Delta|G_{1}\subseteq G\subseteq G_{2}\}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

The intervals
[\mathcal{R}(F_{i}),F_{i}],i=1,2,\cdots,t
partition the shellable complex
\Delta
.

Proof.

Since
F_{1},F_{2},\cdots F_{i}
is a shelling order of
\Delta_{i}
, it is enough to show inductively, that
\Delta_{t}=[\mathcal{R}(F_{t}),F_{t}]\cup\Delta_{t-1}
, and
[\mathcal{R}(F_{t}):F_{t}]\cap\Delta_{t-1}=\emptyset
since
\Delta_{1}=[\mathcal{R}(F_{1}),F_{1}]
and
\mathcal{R}(F_{1})=\emptyset
.

Let
P\in\Delta_{t}-\Delta_{t-1}
, then
P\subseteq F_{t}
. since
\Delta_{t}=\Delta_{t-1}\cup F_{t}
. Also if
x\in\mathcal{R}(F_{t})-P\implies F_{t}-x\in\Delta_{t-1}\text{ and }x\notin P
. Now this implies that
P\subseteq F_{t}-x
and this contradicts the fact that
P\notin\Delta_{t-1}
. This proves that
\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\subseteq P
and hence the first claim is proved. Now if
G\in[\mathcal{R}(F_{t}):F_{t}]\cap\Delta_{t-1},\implies\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\subseteq G\subseteq F_{t}
and
G\in\Delta_{t-1}\implies\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\in\Delta_{t-1}
. If
\mathcal{R}(F_{t})-F_{t}\implies F_{t}\in\Delta_{t-1}
- a contradiction. So,
\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\subsetneq F_{t}
. Hence there exists some
i<t
such that
\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\subset F_{i}\cap F_{t}
. Since
\{F_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}
is a shelling order, so there exists some
k<t
and
x\in F_{t}
such that
\mathcal{R}(F_{t})\subset F_{i}\cap F_{t}\subseteq F_{k}\cap F_{t}=F_{t}-\{x\}
. Observe that,
F_{t}-\{x\}\subseteq F_{k}
for
k<t
. Hence,
F_{t}-\{x\}\in\Delta_{t-1}\implies x\in\mathcal{R}(F_{t})
which is a contradiction.
This completes our proof.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

A poset
\left(P,<\right)
on a set
P
, is a set with an order relation
<
such that the relation is

(1)

Reflexive, i.e.
a<a\ \forall a\in P
.

(2)

Antisymmetric.
a<b
and
b<a\implies

a=b
.

(3)

Transitive.
a<b
and
b<c\implies

a<c
.

To every simplicial complex
\Delta
, one can associate a poset
P(\Delta)
, called the
face poset
of
\Delta
, which is defined to be the poset of non empty faces ordered by inclusions.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
, the power set of its edge set
2^{E}
is an abstract simplicial complex on the edge set
E
. Consider its face poset
P(2^{E})
ordered by natural inclusion. We denote this poset by
\left(P(2^{E}),<\right)
. Given any two elements
s,s^{\prime}\in P(2^{E})
, we say that
s
is covered by
s^{\prime}
, if
s<s^{\prime}
and there does not exist any
p\in P(2^{E})
such that
s<p<s^{\prime}
. We denote the cover relation by
s\lessdot s^{\prime}
.

Example 2.9
.

1
2
3
4

((a))

G=(V,E)

\{1,2,3,4\}
\{1,2,3\}
\{1,2,4\}
\{1,3,4\}
\{2,3,4\}
\{1,2\}
\{1,3\}
\{1,4\}
\{2,3\}
\{2,4\}
\{3,4\}
\{1\}
\{2\}
\{3\}
\{4\}
\emptyset

((b))

Face Poset

Figure 1
.
A graph G and the Hasse diagram of the face poset
P(2^{E})

Consider the graph
G=(V,E)
with the edge set
E=\{1,2,3,4\}
as given in figure
1
. The simplicial complex
2^{E}
and its corresponding face poset is given. Note that by
\emptyset
we mean the spanning subgraph of
G
with no edges. Similarly, for any subgraph
H\in 2^{E}
, we mean the spanning subgraph of
G
with edge set same as
H
.

2.3.
A Short Review of Graph Co-homology

In this subsection we recall the construction of Chromatic homology associated to a graph. There are multiple formulations of Chromatic homology in the literature. In this paper we shall stick to enhanced state formulation of Chromatic homology. Since, our results in this paper involves the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{m}:=\frac{\mathbb{Z}[x]}{\left<x^{m}\right>}
, we shall only focus on
\mathcal{A}_{m}
.

An enhanced state is an ordered pair
\left(H,\epsilon\right)
where
H
is a spanning subgraph of
G
and
\epsilon
is a coloring of the connected components of
H
by the colors
\{1,x,\ldots,x^{m-1}\}
. More precisely, let
H\subset G
is a subgraph with
V(H)=V(G)
and
E(H)\subset E(G)
. If
\mathcal{C}
denotes the collection of connected components of
H
, then
\epsilon
is a map

\epsilon:\mathcal{C}\to\{1,x,\ldots,x^{m-1}\}.

2.3.1.
Chain Groups

Let
C_{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)
denotes the free abelian group generated by all possible enhanced states
\left(H,\epsilon\right)
. We turn this into a graded abelian group where the grading is inherited by the cardinality of
|E(H)|
. Let
n
denote the number of edges of
G
. That is, we have the following decomposition:

\displaystyle C^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)

\displaystyle{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G):

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\{H|\,|E(H)|=i\}}\mathbb{Z}\left<(H,\epsilon)\right>

Essentially
{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}
consists of all possible enhanced spanning subgraphs
H
of
G
where
\lvert E(H)\rvert=i
[See Figure
2
],

Figure 2
.
Chromatic chain groups in terms of enhanced spanning subgraphs for the graph
P_{3}
.

Figure 3
.
Differential between the chain groups
C_{0}(P_{3})
and
C_{1}(P_{3})

2.3.2.
Differential

In this subsection we recall the definition of the differential

d_{i}:{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)\to{C_{i+1}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)

in the Chromatic complex. The differential
d_{i}
has two ingredients
(1.)
multiplication/identity and
(2.)
the sign correction which we describe as follows. The differential
d_{i}
evaluated at each generator is going to look like

d_{i}\left((H,\epsilon)\right)=\sum_{e\notin E(H)}(-1)^{e}\cdot d_{i}^{e}\left((H.\epsilon)\right)

where
d_{i}^{e}
is defined as per-edge maps and is given by

d_{i}^{e}\left((H,\epsilon)\right)=(H\cup e,\epsilon_{e})
.

If an edge
e
is added to the spanning subgraph
H
, then the edge
e
can either connect two distinct connected components of
H
or
e
connect two vertices within the same connected component of
H
.

In the first case [See Figure
3
], if
e
connects two distinct components of
H
say
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
then
\epsilon_{e}
is defined as follows

\displaystyle\epsilon_{e}\left(C\right):

\displaystyle=\epsilon(C)\,\,\text{if}\,\,C\neq C_{i},C_{j}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{e}(C_{i}\cup C_{j}\cup e):

\displaystyle=\epsilon(C_{i})\cdot\epsilon(C_{j})

where
\epsilon(C_{i})\cdot\epsilon(C_{j})
is defined by the algebra multiplication in
\mathcal{A}_{m}
. Thus,
(H,\epsilon_{e})\equiv 0
when
\epsilon(C_{i})\cdot\epsilon(C_{j})=0
in
\mathcal{A}_{m}
.

In the second case, if
e
connects two vertices within the same connected component of
H
say
C_{i}
then
\epsilon_{e}
is defined by identity that is

\displaystyle\epsilon_{e}(C):

\displaystyle=\epsilon(C)\,\,\text{if}\,\,C\neq C_{i}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{e}(C_{i}\cup e):

\displaystyle=\epsilon(C_{i})

Next, we discuss the sign correction
(-1)^{e}
. We start with an well ordering on the edges of
G
say
e_{1}<e_{2}<\ldots<e_{n}
. Then, we have a canonical basis
\mathcal{B}
for the exterior algebra generated by
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}

\bigwedge\left(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n}\right)

given by

\mathcal{B}=\{e_{i_{1}}\wedge e_{i_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{i_{k}}\ \mid\ k,1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n\}
.
Suppose the edge set of H consists of
E(H)=\{e_{k_{1}}<e_{k_{2}}<\ldots<e_{k_{i}}\}
then
(-1)^{e}
is defined by the sign such that

(-1)^{e}e_{k_{1}}\wedge e_{k_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{k_{i}}\wedge e\in\mathcal{B}.

Another description of the sign correction can be given as follows: given
H
, let
H_{e}:=\{e_{k_{i}}:e_{k_{i}}\in E(H)\,\,\text{and}\,\,e_{k_{i}}<e\}
. Then we can also take
(-1)^{e}:=(-1)^{|H_{e}|}
.

A straightforward calculation implies:

Proposition 2.10
.

The differential that is defined above satisfies
d^{2}=0
.

For any
i
, the
i
-th part of the Chromatic chain complex
{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)
inherits an additional grading known as the quantum grading that is

{C_{i}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}{C_{i,j}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G).

Essentially
C

The quantum grading
j:=j(H,\epsilon)
for each enhanced state
(H,\epsilon)
is given by the formula

j(H,\epsilon):=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}i\times|\{C:\epsilon(C)=x^{i}\}|

The bi-graded homology group will be denoted by
{{H_{i,j}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
for homological grading
i
and quantum grading
j
.

2.4.
Broken circuit Complex

Definition 2.11
.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
, the
edge space

\mathcal{E}(G)
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
vector space with the basis
E
. The
Cycle Space

C(G)
is the subspace of
\mathcal{E}(G)
generated by cycles of G. Given
S,T\in 2^{E}
,
S+T:=S\Delta T=S\cup T-S\cap T
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\cdots e_{n}\}
be a fixed ordering on its edges.
A
broken circuit
in
G
is a set
C+e\in\mathcal{E}(G)
, where
C
is a cycle in G and
e
is the least ordered edge in the cycle. We shall use the convention that a broken circuit is the least ordered edge removed from the cycle, instead of the convention used in
[
CS19
]
that, it is the largest ordered edge removed from the cycle. Define
\operatorname{NBC}_{G,\mathcal{O}}
to be the collection of all sets
S\subset E
such that S contains no broken circuits. Let
\operatorname{BC}_{G,\mathcal{O}}
be its compliment, i.e.
\operatorname{BC}_{G,\mathcal{O}}=2^{E}-\operatorname{NBC}_{G,\mathcal{O}}
. Whenever
G
and
\mathcal{O}
is clear from the context then we shall omit it and denote the broken and non broken circuit by just BC and NBC respectively.
Recall from
2.1
that
\mathcal{M}^{c}
denote the set of critical vertices with respect to a given matching
\mathcal{M}
on a directed graph
G
. Given a graph
G=(V,E)
. Let
\lambda(s)
denote the number of connected components in the spanning subgraph
G(s)
and
k(s)
denote the integer partition of
\lvert V\rvert
consisting the sizes of the connected component of
s
.

Lemma 2.12
.

[
CS19
, Lemma 4.3]

Let G = (V, E) be a graph with a fixed ordering of its edges. The face poset
P(2^{E})
has an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{BC}}
in which each matched pair
S\lessdot S^{\prime}
, satisfies
k(S)=k(S^{\prime})
, and
\lambda(S)=\lambda(S^{\prime})
and the corresponding morse complex is given by
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{BC}}^{c}=\operatorname{NBC}
.

Observe that,
\operatorname{NBC}\subset 2^{E}
is a subcomplex of
2^{E}
. The face poset corresponding to it
P(\operatorname{NBC})
is a subset of
P(2^{E})
and hence also a poset. Hence, one can consider the restriction of the chromatic complex to NBC yeilding a co-chain complex
C_{\ast}(\operatorname{NBC},d_{\ast}|_{\operatorname{NBC}})
. Let us denote the homology of this complex by
H_{\ast}(\operatorname{NBC})
. Now using
2.12
and
2.3
Chandler and Sazdanovic proved the following theorem.

Theorem 2.13
.

[
CS19
, Theorem 5.8]

Let
A=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{i}
be a commutative, graded R-algebra with identity such that each
A_{i}
is free of finite rank. For any graph
G=(V,E)
, and any fixed ordering of the edge set
E
,

H_{\ast}(G)\cong H_{\ast}(\operatorname{NBC}).

Theorem
2.13
can be thought of as the categorification of Whitney’s broken circuit theorem for the chromatic polynomial.

In this paper we will be only concerned with
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebras. Observe that Theorem
2.13
can be interpreted in the following way.

Observation 2.14
.

Given an ordering on the edges of a graph
G
, the homology of the chromatic complex over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
,
{{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
is isomorphic to the homology of the subcomplex generated by enhanced NBC subgraphs which we denote by
{{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
. In fact, the acyclic matching provided by them by Theorem
2.3
implies that that
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
is chain homotopy equivalent to
{{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
.

2.5.
Activity word on a spanning tree

Definition 2.15
.

Let
G
be graph with a fixed ordering on its edges. Then for a spanning tree
T
of
G
and for each edge
e\in T
, the
cut-set
of
e
is defined as,
\emph{cut}(T,e)=\{e^{\prime}\in G\mid e^{\prime}\text{ connects }T\setminus e\}
. If
f\not\in T
, then the
cycle
of
f
is defined as
\emph{cyc}(T,f)=\{f^{\prime}\in G\mid f^{\prime}\text{ belongs to the unique cycle in }T\cup f\}
.

Proposition 2.16
.

For any tree T,
e\in\emph{cut}(T,p)\iff p\in\emph{cyc}(T,e).

Proof.

Let
e\in\operatorname{cut}(T,p)
. This implies that
p\in T
and
e\notin T
and
e
connects two disjoint components of
T\setminus p
, say
C_{1},C_{2}
. Now if we insert the edge
p
also, it creates an unique cycle which contains both
p
and
e
. This proves the proposition.
∎

Given an edge
e\in T
, if
e
is the smallest edge in
\emph{cut}(T,e)
, then
e
is said to be an
internally active (live)
edge (
L
) of the tree
T
, otherwise it is said to be an
internally inactive (dead)
edge (
D
). Similarly, if
e\not\in T
and
e
is the smallest edge in
\emph{cyc}(T,e)
, then
e
is said to be
externally active (live)
edge (
l
) of
T
or else it is said to be
externally inactive (dead)
edge (
d
) of T.

So given a spanning tree
T
of a graph
G=(V,E)
, we define an activity word function for an edge
e
,
a_{T}(e):E(G)\rightarrow\{L,D,l,d\}
. The activity word function for the tree
W(T)
is defined to be the formal free product of
a_{T}(e)
in increasing order of edges
e
. We denote the collection of internally inactive edges of a tree by
\operatorname{IN}(T)
, the collection of internally active edges of a tree by
\operatorname{IA}(T)
and the collection of externally inactive edges by
\operatorname{EN}(T)
. Observe that in an NBC tree, there are no externally active edges. In other words, if
T
is an NBC tree and
e\notin T
, then
a_{T}(e)=d
.

1
3
2

((a))

A graph
G
, with a fixed ordering

1
3
2

((b))

A spanning tree
T
of
G
, with
W(T)=LdD

Figure 4
.
Comparison of graph
G
and tree
T

3.
Spanning Tree Model for Chromatic Homology

In this entire section, except subsection 3.6, we consider the chromatic complex of a graph over the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\frac{\mathbb{Z}[x]}{<x^{2}>}
. Also, we consider any graph
G
with a distinguished vertex on it, that we call a root. Although the matching that we provide on the NBC complex depends on the choice of the distinguished vertex on the graph, the chromatic or spanning tree homology is independent of it.
Recall that the non-broken circuit complex NBC is a subcomplex of
2^{E}
with facets as spanning trees that do not contain any broken circuit. In this section, we first provide an acyclic matching on the chromatic complex of a tree, and using this matching, we can provide a matching to the subcomplex NBC for any given graph
G
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a simple graph with a fixed ordering
\mathcal{O}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
on its edges
E
. Let
\lvert V\rvert=k
. Let
T
be any spanning tree of
G
. Then we write
T
as an ordered
(k-1)
-tuple in increasing order of its edges, i.e.,
T=[e_{i_{1}},e_{i_{2}},\dots,e_{i_{k-1}}]
if and only if
e_{i_{1}}<e_{i_{2}}<\dots<e_{i_{k-1}}
.

One can provide an ordering on the facets, i.e. spanning trees, called
lexicographic
ordering, defined as follows:

T_{1}=[e_{i_{1}},e_{i_{2}},\dots,e_{i_{k-1}}]<T_{2}=[e_{j_{1}},e_{j_{2}},\dots,e_{j_{k-1}}]

if and only if
e_{i_{k}}=e_{j_{k}}
for all
k<p
and
e_{i_{p}}<e_{j_{p}}
, for some
p\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

We will provide proofs of some important propositions about NBC trees, which can also be found in a more general setting of matroids in
[
Bjö92
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

The lexicographic ordering is a shelling order of
\operatorname{NBC}
. Thus,
\operatorname{NBC}
is a shellable complex.

Proof.

Let
\{T_{1}<T_{2}<\cdots<T_{p}\}
be a lexicographic ordering on the facets of NBC. Let
T_{i},T_{j}
be any pair of facets such that
T_{i}<T_{j}
.

Let
T_{i}=[e_{t_{1}^{i}},e_{t_{2}^{i}},\cdots e_{t_{k-1}^{i}}]
and
T_{j}=[e_{t_{1}^{j}},e_{t_{2}^{j}},\cdots e_{t_{k-1}^{j}}]
.
Then there exists a smallest integer
l\in\{1,2,..,k-1\}
such that
e_{t_{s}^{i}}=e_{t_{s}^{j}},\forall s<l
and
e_{t_{l}^{i}}<e_{t_{l}^{j}}
. Moreover it follows that
e_{t_{l}^{i}}<e_{t_{s}^{j}}\forall s>l
since,
e_{t_{s}^{j}}<e_{t_{s+1}^{j}}\forall s\in[k-2]
.
Let
T_{k}^{\prime}=T_{j}\cup\{e_{t_{l}^{i}}\}
. Since
T_{j}
is a spanning tree,
T_{k}^{\prime}
contains an unique cycle
C
. Let
T_{k}=T_{k}^{\prime}-\{e_{t_{p}^{j}}\}
, where
e_{t_{p}^{j}}
is an edge in the cycle
C
which is not the least ordered one and not equals to
e_{t_{l}^{i}}
and
p>l
. Otherwise if, the cycle consists of edges
e_{t_{p}^{j}}
only with
p<l
and
e_{t_{l}^{i}}
, that would be a contradiction as
T_{i}
would contain the cycle
C
. Thus, one can always choose such an edge provided the cycle contain three or more elements.

Then it is straightforward from the construction that
T_{k}
is a facet of NBC.
Moreover
T_{j}=[e_{t_{1}^{j}},e_{t_{2}^{j}},\cdots e_{t_{l}^{j}},e_{t_{l+1}^{j}},\cdots e_{t_{k-1}^{j}}]
and
T_{k}=[e_{t_{1}^{j}},e_{t_{2}^{j}},\cdots e_{t_{l-1}^{j}},e_{t_{l}^{i}},\cdots,\hat{e_{t_{p}^{j}}},\cdots e_{t_{k-1}^{j}}]
, where
\hat{e_{t_{p}^{j}}}
is the edge removed from the cycle. Then it follows that
T_{k}<T_{j}
. Then
T_{i}\cap T_{j}\subset T_{k}\cap T_{j}=T_{j}-\{e_{t_{p}^{j}}\}
. Hence, the lexicographic order is a shelling.
∎

Recall from
2.7
that if we have a shellable complex, then we have a partition of the complex.
We shall use this
lexicographic
ordering on NBC to extend the matching on the whole NBC Complex using these partitions.

Proposition 3.2
.

If
T_{1}
is the
\operatorname{NBC}
spanning tree with the smallest lexicographic order then all the internal edges of
T_{1}
are active.

Proof.

Assume there is an internally inactive edge
e\in T_{1}
, then let
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{1},e)
be the minimum edge with
f\neq e
. Consider
T=T_{1}\cup\{f\}\setminus\{e\}
then,
a_{T}(f)=L
and
a_{T}(e)=d
and the activity of the rest of the edges of
T
remains same with that of
T_{1}
. Since,
f<e
so according to lexicographic ordering
T<T_{1}
which contradicts the minimality of
T_{1}
with respect to the lexicographic order.
∎

Also recall that, for a tree
T
, we denote the collection of inactive edges of
T
by
\operatorname{IN}(T)
. NBC spanning trees
T
of a graph
G
are facets of the subcomplex NBC. As discussed in
2.2
,
\mathcal{R}(T)
corresponds to the restriction of

T
induced by this shelling order. Then we have the following lemma:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\{T_{1},T_{2},\cdots T_{k}\}
be collection of NBC spanning tree of a graph
G
, then
\mathcal{R}(T_{i})=\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})
.

Proof.

For
T_{1}
, we have that
\mathcal{R}(T_{1})=\emptyset
, and from proposition
3.2
the thoerem follows. For
i\geq 2
let us recall that
e\in\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\iff T_{i}-\{e\}\in\Delta_{i-1}
. Hence there exists some
j\leq i-1
, such that
T_{i}-\{e\}\in T_{j}
. Thus,
T_{j}=\left(T_{i}-e\right)\cup f
where
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
. Now the edge sets of
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
agree except for
e
and
f
. Since,
T_{j}<T_{i}
, we conclude
f<e
. In other words, this implies
e
must be internally dead. Hence, we conclude
\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\subset\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})
.

In the opposite direction, let us choose
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})
. As
e
is internally dead,
e\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
is not minimal. Thus, there exists
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
,
f<e
and
f
is minimal in the set
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
. We look at the tree
T:=(T_{i}-e)\cup f
. Clearly
T<T_{i}
by the definition of lexicographic ordering. It remains to show that
T
is a NBC tree.

Assume on the contrary that
T
is not a NBC tree. Now
T_{i}-e
is a disjoint union of two trees
T_{i}^{(1)}
and
T_{i}^{(2)}
. If
T
contains a broken circuit of the form
\left(C+g\right)\subset T
, then we note that
f\in C
. Otherwise,
C+g
would be a broken circuit in
T_{i}
contradicting the hypothesis that
T_{i}
is a NBC tree. Thus,
f\in C
and
g<f
. Now
g\notin\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
as
f
is the minimal element in
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
. Hence
g
must connect two vertices in
T_{i}^{(1)}
or in
T_{i}^{(2)}
. This is a contradiction as the broken circuit
C+g
lies in
T_{i}
. Hence,
T
is a NBC tree. By the lexicographic ordering on the NBC trees
T=T_{j}
for some
j<i
.

We essentially get
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})\subset\mathcal{R}(T_{i})
concluding the proof of the proposition.

Figure 5
.
Schematic picture

∎

We shall end this subsection with an important observation which would be crucial in the acyclicity of the NBC complex.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
S\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
be such that
S\cup{e}\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
, then
j\geq i
.

Proof.

If
e\in T_{i}
, then
j=i
. So with out loss of generality, we may assume
e\notin T_{i}
. Let us assume
j<i
. Then, by hypothesis

\mathcal{R}(T_{j})\subset S\cup e\subset T_{j}.

Now, either
e\in\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
or
e\notin\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
. We shall show, both of this cases leads to a contradiction.

If
e\notin\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
, then the first inclusion implies
\mathcal{R}(T_{j})\subset(S\cup e)-e=S
. But then
S\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]\cap[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
, contradicting the fact that they are disjoint.
Next, assume
e\in\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
, this implies
T_{j}-e\in\triangle_{j-1}
where
\triangle_{j-1}
is the complex generated by
T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{j-1}
. Now, we have the following chain of inclusions

S=(S\cup e)-e\subset T_{j}-e\subset\triangle_{j-1}.

Then,
S\in\triangle_{j-1}\cap[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
. The only possibility of such an intersection if
j-1=i
, since
j-1<j<i
, that leads to a contradiction.Hence,
j\geq i.

∎

3.1.
Acyclic matching on chromatic complex of a tree

Let
T
be a tree rooted at the vertex
v_{d}
. Recall that the generators of the chromatic complex of graph correspond to enhanced spanning subgraphs
[
HGR05
]
. Formally, an enhanced spanning subgraph
(H,\epsilon)
of a graph
G
is a spanning subgraph
H
of
G
together with a set theoretic map

\epsilon:C(H)\rightarrow\{1,x\}
where
C(H)
is the set of connected components of
H
.

Recall that, algebraic Morse theory associates a directed weighted graph
G_{C_{\ast}}
to a co-chain complex
C_{\ast}
. Now for a tree
T=(V,E)
, with
n
many edges, we will inductively provide an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{T}
on the chromatic complex of
C_{\ast}(T)
, that is on the graph
G_{C_{\ast}(T)}
. Induction statement is on the number of edges of the rooted tree
T
.

(1)

Base case:
n=1
For a rooted tree
T
, with one edge, let
v_{d}
denote the root vertex. The matching in this case is shown in Figure

.

(3)

Now we provide the matching on the set of remaining enhanced subraphs of

those we denote by

. By induction let

. We can use the matching

. Thus by induction hypothesis, we have a matching on the entire chromatic complex of the tree with n many edges, with only two critical states which are supported at the homological grading

and the with enhancement same as described in (

).

Lemma 3.5
.

The matching
\mathcal{M}_{T}
on the chromatic complex of
T
is acyclic.

Proof.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that there is a directed cycle of enhanced spanning subgraphs

H_{1}\to d_{p}^{e_{1}}(H_{1})\to H_{2}\to d_{p}^{e_{2}}(H_{2})\to H_{3}\to\cdots\to d_{p}^{e_{k}}(H_{k})\to H_{1}

in the Hasse diagram of the chromatic complex of
T
, with matching
\mathcal{M}_{T}
. Let
p
be the homological grading of
H_{i}
, and
d_{p}^{e_{i}}(H_{i})
is obtained by taking the differential with respect to the edge
e_{i}
. (See Figure
8
.)

H_{1}
d_{p}^{e_{1}}(H_{1})
d_{p}^{e_{1}}
H_{2}
d_{p}^{e_{2}}(H_{2})
d_{p}^{e_{2}}

Figure 8
.
A directed cycle in a Hasse diagram

Define a distance function

d:\{v_{d}\}\times V(T)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},\quad d(v_{d},v):=\text{distance of the vertex }v\text{ from the root in the graph }T.

Also, define

(5)

{}d(v_{d},S):=\min\{d(v_{d},v)\mid v\in S\}

Let
v^{(i)}
and
v^{\prime(i)}
be the two endpoints of the edge
e_{i}
, and let their connected components in
C(H_{i})
be denoted by
C_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{i}
, respectively. Without loss of generality, assume that
d(v_{d},C_{i})<d(v_{d},C^{\prime}_{i})
. Let
e^{\prime}_{i}
be the unique edge in
d_{p}^{e_{i}}(H_{i})\setminus H_{i+1}
(the index is taken modulo
k
). In the directed cycle differential by
e^{\prime}_{i}
correspond to the matching arrow between
d_{p}^{e_{i}}(H_{i})
and
H_{i+1}
. Given two edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
of the tree
T
, we say
e<_{\mathcal{M}_{T}}e^{\prime}
or
e
is less than
e^{\prime}
in the matching order if matching by
e
occurs before matching by
e^{\prime}
in the recursive construction of
\mathcal{M}_{T}
. Assume that
e_{1}^{\prime}<_{\mathcal{M}_{T}}e_{2}^{\prime}<_{\mathcal{M}_{T}}\cdots<_{\mathcal{M}_{T}}e_{k}^{\prime}
.

(1)

Case 1:
Assume
e_{i}\neq e^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,2,\dots,k
. Since
e^{\prime}_{1}\neq e_{1}
, the edge
e^{\prime}_{1}
is contained in the subgraph
H_{1}
. But there is a matching arrow from
d_{p}^{e_{k}}(H_{k})
to
H_{1}
via differential along the edge
e^{\prime}_{k}
, which is a contradiction since
e^{\prime}_{1}
is contained in the subgraph
d_{p}^{e_{k}}(H_{k})
and
e^{\prime}_{1}
is the minimum edge in the matching order by assumption.

(2)

Case 2:
Assume
e_{i}=e^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i
. Then, we must have
\epsilon(C_{i})=1,\epsilon(C^{\prime}_{i})=x
, as any other enhancement on these two components would violate the property of a directed cycle in a Hasse diagram (That is, in a directed cycle, a matching arrow must be followed by a distinct differential arrow). Then,
H_{i+1}
and
H_{i}
are the same as spanning subgraphs, and
\epsilon(C_{i+1})=x,\epsilon(C^{\prime}_{i+1})=1
. Also, the enhancement remains unchanged for any other connected component of
H_{i}
.

Define

D_{H_{j}}=\sum_{\{C\in C(H_{j})\mid\epsilon(C)=x\}}d(v_{d},C).

Then, in this case, we have
D_{H_{i+1}}<D_{H_{i}}
. Also, notice that the function
D_{H_{j}}
is non-increasing in
j
. This implies,

D_{H_{1}}\geq D_{H_{2}}\geq D_{H_{3}}>\cdots\geq D_{H_{i}}>D_{H_{i+1}}\geq\cdots\geq D_{H_{k}}\geq D_{H_{1}},

which is a contradiction. Thus, such a cycle cannot exist, and the matching is acyclic.

∎

3.2.
Acyclic matching on the chromatic complex of a graph G

Let
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
Now, we want define an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on the NBC complex of any graph
G
. We would like to extend the acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{T}
for a spanning tree of
G
, so that the critical states of
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
are in one to one correspondence with signed NBC spanning trees of
G
. Again, we fix a vertex
v_{d}
of
G
which will play the role of the root for the NBC spanning trees. Let
\{T_{1}<T_{2}<\cdots<T_{k}\}
be the lexicographic ordering on NBC spanning trees of
G
. We inductively provide a matching
\mathcal{M}_{G}
on the NBC complex of
G
.

Recall from
2.7
that, for a shellable complex
\Delta
with facets
\{F_{1},F_{2},\cdots F_{k}\}
, the intervals
[\mathcal{R}(F_{i}),F_{i}]
partitions
\Delta
. It follows immediately from
3.1
, that for any graph
G
, NBC is a shellable complex. Furthermore, the intervals
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
forms a partition of the complex NBC, where
T_{i}
’s are the spanning trees of
G
and they correspond to the facets of NBC.

Theorem 3.6
.

There exists an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on the complex
{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}(G)}
generated by enhanced NBC spanning subgraphs of any graph
G
. Moreover, the critical states
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
of are in one to one correspondence with the signed NBC spanning trees of
G
.

Figure 9
.
The edges in
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i+1})
are colored red

Proof.

Let us assume
T_{1}<T_{2}<\ldots<T_{k}
denotes the shelling order on the NBC spanning trees of the graph
G
. Our strategy to construct the acyclic matching on the NBC complex involves studying each of the intervals
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
separately. Each interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
will contain exactly two critical states. They shall be denoted by
T_{i}^{+}
and
T_{i}^{-}
. We shall provide an explicit description of
T_{i}^{+}
and
T_{i}^{-}
. We proceed by induction on the indexing set
i
of the NBC spanning trees.

(1)

\mathcal{R}(T_{1})=\phi
. By Proposition 3.2 the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{1}),T_{1}]
can be identified with the complex generated by
T_{1}
. We provide the matching as described in subsection
3.1
.
Thus,
[\mathcal{R}(T_{1}),T_{1}]
contains precisely two the critical states in the homological grading
0
.
H_{c}^{T_{1}^{+}}
and
H_{c}^{T_{1}^{-}}
can be described as follows:

\displaystyle H_{c}^{T_{1}^{+}}

\displaystyle:=\left(H,\epsilon_{+}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad H_{c}^{T_{1}^{-}}

\displaystyle:=\left(H,\epsilon_{-}\right)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{+}(v)

\displaystyle=x\,\,\text{if}\,\,v\neq v_{d}

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\epsilon_{-}(v)

\displaystyle=x\,\,\text{for all vertex}\,\,v

\displaystyle\epsilon_{+}(v_{d})

\displaystyle=1

where
H
denotes the spanning subgraph with no edges. This concludes our base case.

(2)

Inductively, assume we have constructed
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
and
T_{j}^{+}
,
T_{j}^{-}
denotes the two critical states for all
1\leq j\leq i
in
[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
. We extend
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i+1}),T_{i+1}]
. We write the edge set of
T_{i+1}
as disjoint union
E(T_{i+1})=\operatorname{IN}(T_{i+1})\cup\operatorname{IA}(T_{i+1})
where
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i+1})
and
\operatorname{IA}(T_{i+1})
denotes the collection of inactive and active edges of
T_{i+1}
respectively. Note that, by virtue of Proposition 3.3 we have the following:

\mathcal{R}(T_{i+1})=IN(T_{i+1}).

Let
T_{i+1}^{{}^{\prime}}
denotes the tree which is obtained by from
T_{i+1}
by contracting the inactive edges
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i+1})
. Then the formal subcomplex generated by the spanning subgraphs in
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i+1}),T_{i+1}]
can be canonically identified with the formal chromatic complex of the tree
T_{i+1}^{{}^{\prime}}
. For example see the Figure
9
. With this identification, we extend the matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i+1}),T_{i+1}]
as described in subsection
3.1
. Next we explicitly describe the two critical states in
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i+1}),T_{i+1}]
. Let
H^{T_{i+1}}
denotes the spanning subgraph with
E(H_{T_{i+1}})=\operatorname{IN}(T_{i+1})
and
C_{v_{d}}
denotes the connected component of
H^{T_{i+1}}
that contains the root vertex
v_{d}
.
H_{c}^{T_{i+1}^{+}}
and
H_{c}^{T_{i+1}^{-}}
can be described as follows:

\displaystyle H_{c}^{T_{i+1}^{+}}

\displaystyle:=\left(H^{T_{i+1}},\epsilon_{+}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad H_{c}^{T_{i+1}^{-}}

\displaystyle:=\left(H^{T_{i+1}},\epsilon_{-}\right)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{+}(C)

\displaystyle=x\,\,\text{if}\,\,v_{d}\notin C

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\epsilon_{-}(C)

\displaystyle=x\,\,\text{for all components}\,\,C\,\,\text{of}\,\,H_{T_{i+1}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{+}(C_{v_{d}})

\displaystyle=1

This concludes the inductive step.

Finally, the acyclicity of the matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
follows from Lemma
3.5
and
3.4
and the fact that the matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
is defined on each disjoint intervals
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
inductively. So if
i\neq j
and we move through a differential from
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
to
[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
, then we
must have
j\geq i
. Finally the matching edge in
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
can not leave the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
. Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
provides an acyclic matching on
{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}(G)}
.

∎

The matching on the NBC complex of
P_{3}
. The red arrows indicate the matching edges.

3.3.
Spanning Tree Complex

Definition 3.7

(Spanning Tree Complex)
.

For a given graph
G=(V,E)
with a fixed ordering on its edges and a distinguished vertex
v_{d}
(which we will sometimes refer to as the root), we have a one-to-one correspondence between the signed NBC spanning trees
T^{+},T^{-}
of
G
and the critical points of
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
due to Theorem
3.6
.

We formally define the spanning tree complex
({CST_{*,*}(G)},\partial_{ST})
by bijectively identifying it with the Morse complex of the acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
from Theorem
3.6
. Let us denote the collection of signed NBC spanning trees of
G
by
G_{SST}
. We define the gradings:

\begin{cases}i(T^{+}):=i(H_{c}^{T^{+}}),\hskip 28.45274ptj(T^{+}):=j(H_{c}^{T^{+}}),\\
i(T^{-}):=i(H_{c}^{T^{-}}),\hskip 28.45274ptj(T^{-}):=j(H_{c}^{T^{-}}).\end{cases}

where
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
and
H_{c}^{T^{-}}
are critical states corresponding to
T
with respect to the matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
.

The chain groups of the spanning tree complex
({CST_{*,*}(G)},\partial_{ST})
are defined as follows:

{CST_{i,*}(G)}:=\mathbb{Z}\langle T^{\pm}\mid T^{\pm}\in G_{SST},\,i(T^{+})=i(T^{-})=i\rangle.

Observe that
{CST_{i,*}(G)}
can be written as:

{CST_{i,*}(G)}:={{{CST}^{+}}_{i,j}(G)}\bigoplus{{{CST}^{-}}_{i,j+1}(G)},

where
{{{CST}^{+}}_{i,j}(G)}\subset{CST_{i,*}(G)}(G)
is defined as:

{{{CST}^{+}}_{i,j}(G)}:=\mathbb{Z}\langle T^{+}\mid T^{+}\in G_{SST},\,i(T^{+})=i,j(T^{+})=j\rangle.

Similarly,
{{{CST}^{-}}_{i,j+1}(G)}\subset{CST_{i,*}(G)}(G)
is defined as:

{{{CST}^{-}}_{i,j}(G)}:=\mathbb{Z}\langle T^{-}\mid T^{-}\in G_{SST},\,i(T^{-})=i,j(T^{-})=j\rangle.

Observe that
{{{CST}^{+}}_{i,j}(G)}
consists of critical states in the
(i,j)
-th grading such that
\epsilon(C_{\text{root}})=1
,
where
C_{\text{root}}
is the connected component of
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
containing the root
v_{d}
.

Similarly,
{{{CST}^{-}}_{i,j}(G)}
consists of critical states in the
(i,j)
-th grading such that
\epsilon(C_{\text{root}})=x
,
where
C_{\text{root}}
is the connected component of
H_{c}^{T^{-}}
containing the root
v_{d}
.
The gradings follow from Theorem
3.6
.

Until now,
{CST_{i,j}(G)}
has only been described as a bigraded group. The differential of this chain complex,

\partial_{ST}:{CST_{i,*}(G)}\rightarrow{CST_{i+1,*}(G)},

is defined via correspondence with the differential
\partial^{i}_{\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}}
of the algebraic Morse complex.
Later, in Theorem
3.16
, we will show that it has an explicit graph-theoretic description.

For a given graph
G=(V,E)
, we have defined an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
on the complex
{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}(G)}
in Theorem
3.6
. Recall from Section 2.1 that whenever we have an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{C_{\ast}}
for a given cochain complex
\left(C_{\ast},\partial_{\ast}\right)
, we obtain the corresponding Morse complex
\left(C_{\ast}^{\mathcal{M}},\partial_{\ast}^{\mathcal{M}}\right)
, which is generated by the critical vertices with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{C_{\ast}}
. In this case, the corresponding Morse complex is:

C^{\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}(G):=\bigoplus_{i}C^{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(G),

where

C^{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(G)=\mathbb{Z}\langle H_{c}^{T^{\pm}}\in C^{\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}(G)\mid i(H_{c}^{T^{\pm}})=i)\rangle.

Moreover, we can write

C_{i}^{\mathcal{M}}=\bigoplus_{j}C^{\mathcal{M}}_{i,j}(G).

The differential
\partial^{\mathcal{M}}:C^{\mathcal{M}}_{i,j}\rightarrow C^{\mathcal{M}}_{i+1,j}
is defined as follows:

\partial^{\mathcal{M}}(H_{c}^{T^{\pm}})=\sum_{i(H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}})=i+1}\Gamma(H_{c}^{T^{\pm}},H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}}).H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}}

The incidence
\Gamma(H_{c}^{T^{\pm}},H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}})
is defined as the sum of weights of all alternating paths from
H_{c}^{T^{\pm}}
to
H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}}
in
G^{\mathcal{M}}_{C^{*}}
.

Observe that the enhancement of
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
is
1
at the component containing the root
v_{d}
and
x
on all other connected components. Therefore,
j(H_{c}^{T^{+}})=\operatorname{IA}(T)
. The enhancement of
H_{c}^{T^{-}}
is
x
on all connected components which implies
j(H_{c}^{T^{-}})=\operatorname{IA}(T)+1
. Let,
n
be the number of vertices of
G
. Then We have,

i(H_{c}^{T^{-}})+j(H_{c}^{T^{-}})=\operatorname{IN}(T)+\operatorname{IA}(T)+1=n

and

i(H_{c}^{T^{+}})+j(H_{c}^{T^{+}})=\operatorname{IN}(T)+\operatorname{IA}(T)=n-1.

Since the differential preserves the quantum grading, it follows that
\partial_{ST}
vanishes on
H_{c}^{T^{-}}
. When evaluated at
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
, we obtain:

\partial_{\mathcal{M}}(H_{c}^{T^{+}})=\sum_{i(H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}})=i+1}\Gamma(H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}})H_{c}^{T^{\prime\pm}}.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between the generators of
{CST_{{}_{,}i,j}(G)}(G)
and the generators of
C_{i,j}^{\mathcal{M}}
, leading to a
\mathbb{Z}
-module isomorphism between them. The differential

\partial_{ST}:{CST_{{}^{,}i,j}(G)}\rightarrow{CST_{{}^{,}i+1,j}(G)}

can then be described as:

\partial_{ST}(T^{+})=\sum_{i(T^{\prime-})=i+1}[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):T^{\prime-}]T^{\prime-},

where the incidence
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):T^{\prime-}]
is given by
\Gamma(H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{c}^{T^{\prime-}})
.

Theorem 3.8

(Spanning tree model for chromatic homology)
.

The spanning tree complex
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
and the chromatic complex
{C_{*,*}(G)}
are chain homotopy equivalent. In particular,

{HST_{i,j}(G)}\cong{H_{i,j}(G)}.

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from Theorem
3.6
and Observation
2.14
.
∎

Now, we will take the graded Euler characteristic of
{CST_{*,*}(G)}
and show that it gives us the spanning tree expansion of the chromatic polynomial.

Proof of Corollary
1.2
.

Observe that, the number of inactive edges gives the homological grading of a spanning tree. In other words, Consequently, for a spanning tree
T
with internal activity
i
, the homological grading is
n-i-1
.

Furthermore, in this setting, the tree
T^{+}
has quantum grading
i
, while the tree
T^{-}
has quantum grading
i+1
.

Since the internal activity of NBC spanning trees satisfy
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we obtain the following expression for the graded Euler characteristic:

\displaystyle\chi_{q}({CST_{*,*}(G)})

\displaystyle=\chi_{q}({{{CST}^{+}}_{*,*}(G)})+\chi_{q}({{{CST}^{-}}_{*,*}(G)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}q^{i}(-1)^{n-i-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}q^{i+1}(-1)^{n-i-1}

\displaystyle=(q+1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-i-1}t_{i}q^{i}(-1)^{n-i-1}

\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}(\lambda-1)^{i}(-1)^{n-i-1}\quad\text{(Substituting }\lambda=1+q\text{)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}(1-\lambda)^{i}

Hence,

P(G;\lambda)=(-1)^{n-1}\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}t_{i}(1-\lambda)^{i}

∎

Another immediate corollary of the spanning tree model is the fact that chromatic homology is supported on the diagonals
i+j=n
and
i+j=n-1
.
The generators of
{{{CST}^{+}}_{i,j}(G)}
satisfy
i+j=n-1
and the generators of
{{{CST}^{-}}_{i,j}(G)}
satisfy
i+j=n
.

Corollary 3.9
.

The chromatic homology
{H_{i,j}(G)}
of a graph
G
is supported on the two diagonals
i+j=n
and
i+j=n-1
, where
n
is the number of vertices of
G
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the preceding discussion.
∎

3.4.
Combinatorial form of the differential of the spanning tree complex

Now we want to provide an explicit formula to compute the incidence
[T,T^{\prime}]
in the differential
\partial_{ST}
, that will help significantly to compute the homology of the spanning tree complex.

We start with a few basic observations which would be essential for the combinatorial description of the differential in the algebraic Morse complex. First, we prove an useful property relating the cutsets of the trees obtained by basis exchange.

Proposition 3.10
.

Given two spanning trees
T_{i},T_{j}
with
T_{j}=(T_{i}\cup\{f\})\setminus\{e\}
. If
\alpha\not\in C=\operatorname{cyc}(T_{i}\cup f)
then,
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},\alpha)=\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},\alpha)
.

Proof.

Suppose
h\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},\alpha)
then
h
connects
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
where,
T_{i}\setminus\alpha=C_{1}\sqcup C_{2}
. Now without loss of generality assume that
e\in C_{1}
then,
C\setminus f\subset C_{1}
since,
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\phi
. Now,

T_{j}\setminus\alpha=((T_{i}\cup f)\setminus e)\setminus\alpha=((C_{1}\cup f)\setminus e)\sqcup C_{2}

Now as
\alpha\not\in C
so,
h\neq e
and thus,
h
connects
(C_{1}\cup f)\setminus e)
and
C_{2}
which implies
h\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},\alpha)
and hence,
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},\alpha)\subset\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},\alpha)
. Similarly, one can show that
\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},\alpha)\subset\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},\alpha)
since
h\neq f
and thus, we have our desired result.
∎

Next, we prove another important observation that is crucial for the combinatorial description of the differential.

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose
T_{i}<T_{j}
are two NBC trees such that
\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
then
T_{j}=\left(T_{i}-e\right)\cup f
where
e
is the maximal internally live edge in the unique cycle in
T_{i}\cup f.
Moreover,
\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f=\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
.

Proof.

We note that
f\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{j})
. Otherwise
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})=\left(\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})\cup f\right)-f\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]
by virtue of Proposition 3.3. It contradicts the fact that
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]\cap[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]=\phi
. Thus, the added edge
f
must belongs to
\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
.

Let
T_{k}
denotes the tree as described in the hypothesis that is
T_{k}
is obtained by removing the maximal internally live edge from the unique cycle in
T_{i}\cup f
. We show that such
T_{k}
must always exists and
T_{k}
is an NBC tree. We start with the following observations:

•

Let
C
denotes the unique cycle in
T_{i}\cup f
. Then
C
must contain at least one internally live edge in
T_{i}
.

•

T_{k}
must be an NBC tree.

The first observation is trivial because otherwise
C\subset\left(\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})\cup f\right)=\left(\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f\right)\subset T_{j}
. The equality follows from Proposition 3.3. It contradicts the fact
T_{j}
is a tree. Thus, we can assume such
T_{k}
must exists.

Next, if
C
contains more than one internally live edge in
T_{i}
, then by the construction of
T_{k}
, it must be an NBC tree. So, without loss of generality we may assume that the cycle
C
contains a unique internally live edge
\alpha
of
T_{i}
. That is

\alpha\in\operatorname{IA}(T_{i}).

Moreover for the sake of contradiction we may also assume
\alpha
is minimal in the cycle
C
that is
C-\alpha
is a broken circuit. But then

C-\alpha\subset\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f\subset T_{j}

where
T_{j}
is an NBC tree. Thus,
\alpha
can not be minimal in
C
. Hence,
T_{k}
must be an NBC tree.

To finish the proposition we show
T_{k}
must be equal to
T_{j}
. Note that
f\in T_{k}
must be internally dead that is
f\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})
. Otherwise,

T_{k}-f=T_{i}-e\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]\cap[\mathcal{R}(T_{k}),T_{k}]

Thus,
f\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})
. If
f\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})\subset\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})\cup f
, then

\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})\cup f=\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f\in[\mathcal{R}(T_{k}),T_{k}]\cap[\mathcal{R}(T_{j}),T_{j}]

concluding
k=j.

Thus, we may assume

\left(\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})-f\right)\not\subset\operatorname{IN}(T_{i}).

Thus, there exists
\alpha\in T_{i}\cap T_{k}=T_{i}-e=T_{k}-f
such that
\alpha\in\operatorname{IA}(T_{i})
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})
. In other words, some internally live edge in
T_{i}
turned internally dead in
T_{k}
. We shall show that it leads to a contradiction.

If
\alpha\notin C
, the unique cycle in
T_{i}\cup f
, then by Proposition
3.10
we have the following:

\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},\alpha)=cut(T_{k},\alpha)

Hence, we may assume
\alpha\in C
is internally active in
T_{i}
. Then, by the choice of
e
, we must have

\alpha<e\,\,\text{and}\,\,e<f.

The first inequality follow from the fact that
e
is the maximal among the internally live edges of
C
in
T_{i}
. The second inequality follows from the fact that
e
in internally live and
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
.

Now
\alpha\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})
implies that there exists
\beta\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{k},\alpha)
such that
\beta<\alpha
. Combining all the inequality we get

\beta<\alpha<e<f.

Note that,
\beta\notin\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
as
e
is the minimal edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
. Thus,
\beta
must connect two vertices in
T_{i}^{(1)}
or
T_{i}^{(2)}
depending on whether
\alpha
belongs to
T_{i}^{(1)}
or
T_{i}^{(2)}
as in
5
. In both cases, this contradicts the hypothesis

\alpha\in\operatorname{IA}(T_{i}).

This finishes the argument that
j=k
.

Finally, observe that if any edge
x\in\mathcal{R}(T_{i})=\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})
, then it is not the minimum edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},x)
. Now, either
x\notin\operatorname{cyc}(T_{i},f)
, in which case
\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},x)=\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},x)
, or
x\in\operatorname{cyc}(T_{i},f)
, in which case

\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},x)\setminus\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},x)\subseteq\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e).

In the second case, observe that
e
is live in
T_{i}
, so it is the minimum edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T_{i},e)
. Also, since
e\in\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},x)
, it follows that
x
is not the minimum edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T_{j},x)
. Hence,
x\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{j})=\mathcal{R}(T_{j})
. Therefore, it follows that

\mathcal{R}(T_{i})\cup f=\mathcal{R}(T_{j}).

∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Given two NBC spanning trees
T,T^{\prime}
with
T<T^{\prime}
, the incidences satisfy the following conditions:

[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):T^{\prime+}]=[\partial_{ST}(T^{-}):T^{\prime-}]=[\partial_{ST}(T^{-}):T^{\prime+}]=0.

The incidence
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):T^{\prime-}]
is nonzero only if

T^{\prime}=(T-e)\cup f,

where
f
is an external edge in
T
and
e
is the maximal internally live edge in the unique cycle in
T\cup f
.

Proof.

As observed earlier, the generators are supported in the diagonals
i(T^{+})+j(T^{+})=n-1
and
i(T^{-})+j(T^{-})=n-1
. Since the differential must preserve the quantum grading (
j
-grading) and increase the homological grading(
i
-grading) by
1
, it immediately follows that for any two NBC spanning trees
T,T^{\prime}
with
T<T^{\prime}
, we have

[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):T^{\prime+}]=[\partial_{ST}(T^{-}):T^{\prime-}]=[\partial_{ST}(T^{-}):T^{\prime+}]=0.

For the final conclusion, observe that if in some alternating path in the Morse complex, a differential on an external edge
f
takes an enhanced spanning subgraph
(\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon)
to an enhanced spanning subgraph
(\operatorname{IN}(T)\cup f,\epsilon^{\prime})
in some interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{k}),T_{k}]
, then by Proposition
3.11
,
T_{k}
is uniquely determined. Moreover,

T_{k}=(T-e)\cup f,

where
f
is an external edge in
T
and
e
is the maximal internally live edge in the unique cycle in
T\cup f
. Since
i(T_{k})=i(T)+1
and
\operatorname{IN}(T_{k})=\operatorname{IN}(T)\cup f
, it again follows by Proposition
3.11
that the alternating path must necessarily terminate on some critical enhanced spanning subgraph belonging to the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{k}),T_{k}]
. Otherwise, the homological grading(
i
-grading) would increase by more than
1
. Thus, it follows that
T^{\prime}
is necessarily
T_{k}
, and the conclusion follows.

∎

From now onwards, we shall denote any enhanced spanning subgraph
(H,\epsilon)
in a partition
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
by
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
. Recall from Subsection
2.1
that by
\mathcal{P}(H_{\epsilon}^{T},F_{\epsilon}^{T})
we denote the collection of all alternating paths from
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
to
F_{\epsilon}^{T}
in
G_{C_{\ast}}^{\mathcal{M}}
. In the following definition, we define the sets of two types of alternating paths that we will require.

Definition 3.13

(Alternating paths in the chromatic complex)
.

Given a graph
G
, consider any two enhanced spanning subgraphs
H_{\epsilon},F_{\epsilon}
. Then we define the following:

(1)

\mathcal{P}^{\downarrow}(H_{\epsilon},F_{\epsilon})
:= Number of alternating paths from
H_{\epsilon}
to
F_{\epsilon}
through enhanced spanning subgraphs , where the paths begin with a non-matched edge and
i(H_{\epsilon})+1=i(F_{\epsilon})
.

(2)

\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{\epsilon},F_{\epsilon})
:= Number of alternating paths from
H_{\epsilon}
to
F_{\epsilon}
through enhanced spanning subgraphs, where the paths begin with a non matched edge and
i(H_{\epsilon})=i(F_{\epsilon})
.

Proposition 3.14
.

\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{\epsilon}^{T},F_{\epsilon}^{T})\in\{0,1\}
for all enhanced spanning subgraphs
H_{\epsilon}^{T},F_{\epsilon}^{T}\in[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
.

Proof.

We prove the statement by induction on the number of edges of
T
.

For
|E(T)|=1
, the statement follows directly from the matching
\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

Now, assume that the statement holds for all trees with
|E(T)|<n
. We will prove it for
|E(T)|=n
. Suppose there exist at least two alternating paths,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, between
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
and
F_{\epsilon}^{T}
such that
\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{\epsilon}^{T},F_{\epsilon}^{T})\geq 2
.

By the induction hypothesis, at least one of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
must contain a directed edge
H_{z}^{T}\leftrightarrows dH_{z}^{T}
, where the matching occurs due to the edge
e_{n}
, the smallest ordered leaf in
T
.

Now, either
F_{\epsilon}^{T}={H^{\prime}_{z}}^{T}
or
F_{\epsilon}^{T}
appears immediately after
{H^{\prime}_{z}}^{T}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.

•

If
F_{\epsilon}^{T}={H^{\prime}_{z}}^{T}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{2}
must also contain the same directed edge, leading to at least two alternating paths from
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
to
dH_{\epsilon}^{T}
, which is a contradiction.

•

If
F_{\epsilon}^{T}
appears immediately after
H^{\prime}_{z}{}^{T}
, then we must have
H_{\epsilon}^{T}=H^{\prime}_{z}{}^{T}
, implying
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Thus, the result holds for any spanning tree
T
.
∎

For any spanning tree
T
and a vertex
v
of
G
we have a unique path in
T
between the root
v_{d}
and
v
. Let us denote this unique path by
P_{v}(T)
. Let
C
be a connected component of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
. Then, we can travel from
v_{d}
to
C
in
T
via a unique alternating path in
{C_{*,*}(G)}
.

.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
T
be a spanning tree of
G
, and let
C
be a connected component of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
. Consider an enhancement
\epsilon_{C}
that assigns
1
to
C
and
x
to all other connected components of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
. Let
C_{\text{root}}
be the unique connected component of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
containing the root vertex
v_{d}
. Then,

\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{\epsilon}^{T})=1

for some enhanced spanning subgraph
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
if and only if
H_{\epsilon}^{T}=(\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon_{C})
for some connected component
C
of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
.

Proof.

Let
C(H_{c}^{T^{+}})=\{C_{\text{root}},C_{1},\dots,C_{k}\}
be the connected components of
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
, where
C_{\text{root}}
is the component containing the root
v_{d}
. Consider the unique path
P_{C}
from
C_{\text{root}}
to
C
in the tree
T^{\prime}
, obtained by contracting the internally inactive edges of
T
.

Any alternating path starting from
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
must begin with a non-matched directed edge and can only arise due to an internally active edge
f_{1}
that is incident to the component
C_{\text{root}}
, since
C_{\text{root}}
is the only component in
\operatorname{IN}(T)
where there exists an enhancement
\epsilon
satisfying
\epsilon(C_{\text{root}})=1
. Let
C_{1}
be the other component of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
that merges with
C_{\text{root}}
upon the inclusion of
f_{1}
. Denote by
d^{f_{1}}(H_{c}^{T^{+}})
the enhanced state resulting from the inclusion of
f_{1}
.

The next directed edge in this path must be a matching edge, say
m_{1}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{NBC}}
, this matching
m_{1}
must occur solely due to the internally active edge
f_{1}
. Observe that
m_{1}(d^{f_{1}}(H_{c}^{T^{+}}))
is an enhanced state with the same homological grading as
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
. Thus, there exists a path in
T
from
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
to
(\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon(C_{1}))
via
f_{1}
.

This alternating path can continue through a sequence of internally active edges
\{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{i}\}
of
T
along
P_{C}
(See Figure
11
). Therefore,

\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{\epsilon}^{T})=1.

This also establishes the converse, as we have identified the necessary criterion that
H_{\epsilon}^{T}
must satisfy in order for
\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{\epsilon}^{T})=1
, ensuring the result holds.
∎

To state our theorem about the description of the differential. First, we introduce some notation. Let
v\in V(G)
and
T
is a spanning tree of
G
. Then,
L(P_{v}(T))
denote the number of internally active edges in the path
P_{v}(T)
and
\xi(T,e_{D})
denote the number of edges in
\operatorname{IN}(T)
that are less than
e_{D}
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Suppose
T<T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}=T\cup\{e\}\setminus\{f\}
with
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime})\setminus\operatorname{IN}(T)
. Then the incidence between two critical states
H_{c}^{T^{+}},H_{c}^{T^{\prime-}}
is completely determined by its activity word
W(T)
and
W(T^{\prime})
. In fact, if
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be the end points
e
. Then,

[{\partial_{ST}}(T^{+}),{T^{\prime}}^{-}]={(-1)}^{\xi(T,e)}({(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{1}}(T))}+{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{2}}(T))}).

For any other pair of trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
, we have
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):{T^{\prime}}^{-}]=0
. Therefore, for any two trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
,
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):{T^{\prime}}^{-}]\in\{0,2,-2\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.12
there is exactly one less internally inactive edge in
T
than
T^{\prime}
and hence, there is a pair of edge
(e,f)
such that
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime})
and
f
is the smallest edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T^{\prime},e)
. So,
T^{\prime}=T\cup\{e\}\setminus\{f\}
.

v_{d}
v_{2}
v_{1}
L
L

L
e
L
T^{+}

\partial_{ST}
v_{d}

.

.

Figure 14

Now an alternating path
\mathcal{P}
between
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
and
H_{c}^{T^{\prime-}}
will consist of a pair of enhanced spanning subgraphs
(S_{T},E_{T})
, where
S_{T}=H_{c}^{T^{+}}
,
E_{T}\in[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
with
\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(S_{T},E_{T})=1
. When
\mathcal{P}
travels from
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
to
[\mathcal{R}(T^{\prime}),T^{\prime}]
, at least one of the end vertices of
e
in
E(T)
must have
1
as their enhancement, and thus, by Lemma
3.15
,
E(T)
either corresponds to
P_{v_{1}}(T)
or
P_{v_{2}}(T)
(See Figures
12
,
13
, and
14
, in which case we get
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):{T^{\prime}}^{-}]=0
). For each such choice, if we apply the merge map at
e
, then we will reach the critical state
H_{c}^{T^{\prime-}}
. Thus, there are only two alternating paths between the critical states
H_{c}^{T^{+}}
and
H_{c}^{T^{\prime-}}
.

To determine the incidence
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):{T^{\prime}}^{-}]
, we will need to compute the weight of the alternating paths. Now observe that, using algebraic Morse theory, each matching edge contributes a
-1
factor to the weight, and finally, when we apply the differential using the edge
f
, a weight factor of
{(-1)}^{\xi(T,e)}
is multiplied. Therefore, the weight of the alternating path corresponding to
P_{v_{1}}(T)
is

{(-1)}^{\xi(T,e)}{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{1}}(T))}

and the weight corresponding to
P_{v_{2}}(T)
is

{(-1)}^{\xi(T,e)}{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{2}}(T))}.

By summing up, we obtain

[{\partial_{ST}}(T^{+}),{T^{\prime}}^{-}]={(-1)}^{\xi(T,e)}({(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{1}}(T))}+{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{2}}(T))}).

Now, finally, from the previous lemmas of this subsection, it follows that for any pair of spanning trees
(T,T^{\prime})
that do not satisfy the hypothesis of this theorem, we have
[\partial_{ST}(T^{+}):{T^{\prime}}^{-}]=0
.

∎

Using Theorem
3.16
, we can write a more neat expression of the spanning tree differential. If
e
is an external edge of a NBC spanning tree
T
, then denote the collection of live edges in
\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)
by
L(\operatorname{cyc}(T,e))
and notice that

|L(\operatorname{cyc}(T,e))|\equiv L(P_{v_{1}}(T))+L(P_{v_{2}}(T))\equiv({(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{1}}(T))}+{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{2}}(T))})\pmod{2}

Also, observe that there is a unique NBC spanning tree
T^{\prime}=T\cup\{e\}\setminus\{f\}
where the external edge
f
is inactive and
i(T^{\prime})=i(T)+1
if such a tree exists. It is obtained removing the largest internally active edge in
\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)
.

Definition 3.17

(Trees in the image)
.

Given a tree
T
and an edge
e\in\operatorname{EN}(T)
, define

\psi_{e}(T):=T\cup\{e\}\setminus\{\max(L(\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)))\}.

There also a dual notation of the above map
\psi_{e}
, which we define below as it will be used later.

Definition 3.18

(Trees in the preimage)
.

Given a tree
T
and an edge
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T)
, define

\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T):=T\cup\{\min(\operatorname{cut}(T,e)\}\setminus\{e\}.

Lemma 3.19
.

Suppose
T
is an NBC spanning tree with
i(T)\geq 1
. Then there exists
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T)
such that
\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T)
is an NBC spanning tree satisfying

i(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T))=i(T)-1.

Moreover,
\psi_{e}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T))=T
.

Proof.

Our candidate for
e
will be the smallest edge in
\operatorname{IN}(T)
. Suppose for contradiction
i(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T))\neq i(T)-1
then, there exists
e^{\prime}\in T\cap\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T)
for which
a_{T}(e^{\prime})\neq a_{\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T)}(e^{\prime})
. Let us assume
f=\min\{\operatorname{IN}(\operatorname{cut}(T,e))\}
then we have the following cases:

Case 1:
Suppose
a_{T}(e^{\prime})=L
and
a_{\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T)}(e^{\prime})=D
. Then
e\in\operatorname{cut}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T),e^{\prime})
and
e<e^{\prime}
, but then
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T,e^{\prime})
and
e^{\prime}<f
. Hence, we have
e<f
which is a contradiction.

Case 2:
Suppose
a_{T}(e^{\prime})=D
and
a_{\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T)}(e^{\prime})=L
. Then
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T,e^{\prime})
and
f<e^{\prime}
, but then
e\in\operatorname{cut}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T),e^{\prime})
which implies
e^{\prime}<e
and this contradicts the minimality of
e
.

In order to show
\psi_{e}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T))=T
, we need show that
f=\min(\operatorname{cut}(T,e))=\max(L(\operatorname{cyc}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T),e)))
. Suppose there exists
g>f
with
g\in L(\operatorname{cyc}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T),e))
then, we have
f<g<e
but since,
f\in\operatorname{cut}(T,g)
so,
g\in\operatorname{IN}(T)
but this contradicts the fact that
e
is the smallest edge in
\operatorname{IN}(T)
.
∎

We also define the following function for any vertex
v
incident to edge
e

Definition 3.20
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
e
be an external edge of
T
. Define the function
s_{e}(T)
as follows:

s_{e}(T)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{L(P_{v}(T))},&\text{if }|L(\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)|\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\
0,&\text{if }|L(\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)|\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}

It may seem that
s_{e}(T)
depends on the choice of
v
but the parity of
L(P_{v}(T))
does not depend on
v
and hence,
s_{e}(T)
is independent of the choice of
v
. Let,
\operatorname{NBC}(G)
be the collection of NBC trees of
G
with respect to the fixed ordering. Then, we have following formula for the spanning tree differential.

(6)

\partial_{ST}(T^{+})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\operatorname{EN}(T)\\
\psi_{e}(T)\in\operatorname{NBC}(G)\\
i(\psi_{e}(T))=i(T)+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\xi(T,e)}2s_{e}(T)\cdot{\psi_{e}(T)}^{-}.

Remark 3.21
.

It should be noted that if
e
is the smallest edge in
\operatorname{IN}(T)
then,
\psi_{e}(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T))=T
.

Now, we will prove a theorem that will help us understand the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
torsions in the chromatic homology.

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
G
be a connected graph with an ordering
\omega
on the edge set. If any NBC spanning tree
T
satisfies
\partial_{ST}(T^{+})=0
and the homological grading of
T^{+}
is
i>0
, then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
summand at the
i
’th homological grading.

As a consequence, if
h(\omega)
denotes the homological grading of the maximum NBC spanning tree with respect to the lexicographical order then there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
summand at the homological grading
h(\omega)
.

Proof.

Assume that
T^{+}\in{CST^{+}}_{i}(G)
with
i>0
and
\partial_{ST}(T^{+})=0
. Since
T
has at least one dead edge, then according to Lemma
3.19
we choose
D_{f}
to be the minimum dead edge of
T
, and we have
\psi^{\prime}_{D_{f}}(T)
where
d_{e}
is the minimum edge in
\operatorname{cut}(T,D_{f})
. Let
C
be the cycle consisting edges of
\operatorname{cyc}(\psi^{\prime}_{D_{f}}(T),D_{f})
and
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be the endpoints of
d_{e}
. If there were an even number of live edges in the cycle
C
, then we have

L(P_{v_{1}}(T))\equiv L(P_{v_{2}}(T))\pmod{2}
. Then, by Theorem
3.16
, there exists a tree
T_{2}>T
such that

[{\partial_{ST}}(T^{+}),{T_{2}}^{-}]={(-1)}^{\xi(T,f)}({(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{1}}(T))}+{(-1)}^{L(P_{v_{2}}(T))})\neq 0.

v_{d}

{\psi^{\prime}_{D_{f}}(T)}^{+}

\partial_{ST}

v_{2}

Figure 15
.

[{\partial_{ST}}({\psi^{\prime}_{D_{f}}(T)}^{+}),{T}^{-}]=\pm 2

Hence,
C
must have an odd number of live edges. Thus, by Theorem
3.16
, we have
[{\partial_{ST}}({\psi_{D_{f}}(T)}^{+},{T}^{-}]=\pm 2
. It follows that
{\partial_{ST}}({\psi^{\prime}_{D_{f}}(T)}^{+})=2X
, where
X=\pm T^{-}+\sum_{k\in I}\pm{T_{k}}^{-}
(Here,
I
is an indexing set that is possibly empty, and the homological grading of each

for

is
i
). Since
{\partial_{ST}}(X)=0
, it follows that chromatic homology has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
summand at homological grading
i
.

Let
T_{\omega}
be the NBC tree in highest lexicographical order. As
{\partial_{ST}}({T_{\omega}}^{+})=0
, it follows that there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
summand at
h(\omega)
.
∎

Remark 3.23
.

It should be noted that there is also a unique NBC spanning tree
T_{max}
at the highest homological grading. This follows from our construction in Theorem
4.5
. However, it need not be the same as
T_{\omega}
for all edge orderings
\omega
.

The fact that
h_{max}=v-b-1
for a connected graph with
v
vertices and
b
blocks was established in
[
SS18
]
for the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
algebra. It also follows from Theorem
4.5
, which shows that
h_{max}=v-b-1
more generally for the
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra.

.

Let us compute the spanning tree homology for an

4.
Spanning tree model for chromatic homology over
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra and its applications

Throughout the section we will denote the chromatic complex with underlying algebra
\mathcal{A}_{m}
by
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
and its homology will be denoted by
{{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
. We will use the same technique to provide acyclic matching on the Hasse diagram of
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
which was discussed in subsections
3.1
and
3.2
.

It is not hard to see that the acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{G}
on the chromatic complex over the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
algebra can be generalized to give an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{G,m}
for the chromatic complex over the
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra for any
m\geq 2
. We provide a sketch for completeness.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
G
be a connected simple graph with a fixed ordering on the edge set. For any
m\geq 2
, there exists an acyclic matching matching
\mathcal{M}_{G,m}
on the subcomplex
{C_{\operatorname{NBC}}(G)}
of
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
generated by enhanced NBC spanning subgraphs of any graph
G
.
Let
v
be the number of vertices of the graph
G
. Moreover, there are ,
m{(m-1)}^{|C(\operatorname{IN}(T))|-1}
number of critical points for any NBC spanning tree
T
of
G
where
|C(\operatorname{IN}(T))|
denotes the number of connected components of the subgraph
\operatorname{IN}(T)
.

Proof.

Recall that the generators of the chromatic complex
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
are enhanced spanning subgraphs
(H,\epsilon)
of a graph
G
, where
H
is a spanning subgraph of
G
together with a set-theoretic map

\epsilon:C(H)\rightarrow\{1,x,\dots,x^{m-1}\}
, where
C(H)
is the set of connected components of
H
. Now let us first provide an acyclic matching on the Hasse diagram of
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
for each spanning tree
T
of
G
.

Let
T^{\prime}
be the tree obtained from
T
by contracting the internally inactive edges of
T
. Thus, each enhanced spanning subgraphs of
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
can be paired off with enhanced spanning subgraphs of
[\mathcal{R}(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}]
. We inductively define the matching
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{G,m}
based on the number of edges of
T^{\prime}
as follows:

(1)

Base case:
For a rooted tree
T
with one edge, let
v_{d}
denote the root vertex. The matching in this case is shown in Figure
19
.

Here,

. The critical enhanced spanning subgraphs in this case are the pairs
\{H_{c},\epsilon_{c}\}
, where
H_{c}
is the graph with two isolated vertices
\{v_{d},v_{1}\}
and
\epsilon_{c}(v_{d})\in\{1,x,\cdots,x^{m-1}\}
and
\epsilon_{c}(v_{1})\in\{x,\cdots,x^{m-1}\}
. Moreover, it is clear that this matching is acyclic.

(2)

Inductive step:
Assume that for any tree with
|E(T)|\leq n-1
, we have the matching
\mathcal{M}^{T_{n-1}}_{G,m}
. Then for any tree
T
with

, let

be the least ordered leaf of

. Then denote

such that each of them has a connected component containing
v_{n}
which has a vertex other than
v_{n}
. For

, let

, where

is the edge incident to

and
\epsilon^{\prime}(v_{n})=1
. So, we pair
(H,\epsilon)
with
(H^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
. For the rest of the enhanced spanning subgraphs not in
\mathcal{V}_{n}
, we use the matching
\mathcal{M}^{T_{n-1}}_{G,m}
to pair them off. The matching
\mathcal{M}^{T_{n}}_{G,m}
is illustrated in the following Figure

.
