Thus, the critical enhanced spanning subgraphs obtained from
\mathcal{M}^{T_{n}}_{G,m}
are pairs
\{(H_{c},\epsilon_{c})\}
, where
H
is the graph with
n
isolated vertices where each such vertex is either a component containing internally inactive edges of
T_{n}
or just an isolated vertex, and
\epsilon_{c}
is defined as follows:

\epsilon_{c}(C):=\begin{cases}x^{i}&v_{d}\in C,\>i\in\{0,\cdots,m-1\}\\
x^{j}&v_{d}\not\in C,\>j\in\{1,\cdots,m-1\}\end{cases}

Hence there are,
m{(n-1)}^{m-1}
critical points for a NBC tree. By a similar argument in Theorem
3.5
, it can be showed that this matching is acyclic and can be extended to an acyclic matching
\mathcal{M}_{G,m}
for the chromatic complex over the
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra following arguments similar to Subsection
3.2
. This is done, by matching using internally active edges of
T_{i}
for enhanced spanning subgraphs in the interval
[\mathcal{R}(T_{i}),T_{i}]
. It is then straightforward to see that there are exactly
m{(m-1)}^{|C(\operatorname{IN}(T))|-1}
number of critical points for any NBC spanning tree
T
of
G
.

∎

Now, we introduce the notation to classify the critical points of the Morse complex
C^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}_{\mathcal{M}}(G)

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
T
be a spanning tree of
G
. Let
C(\operatorname{IN}(T))
denote the set of components such that each of those consists of internal inactive edges of the tree. There is a distinguished component containing the root
v_{d}
which we denote by
R_{C}
. Then the set of critical states correspond to
T
are in one to one correspondence with maps
f:C(\operatorname{IN}(T))\rightarrow\{1,x,\cdots,x^{m-1}\}
that satisfy
f(C(\operatorname{IN}(T))\setminus{R_{C}})\subseteq\{x,\cdots,x^{m-1}\}
. Therefore, in
H_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}^{\mathcal{M}}(G)
there are
m{(m-1)}^{|C(\operatorname{IN}(T))|-1}
critical points for each spanning tree
T
of
G
.

We denote a generator of
{{CST_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
by
T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n})}
, where
n
is number of connected components of the critical enhanced spanning subgraph in
{{C_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
corresponding to
T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n})}
and each
i_{k}
represents the enhancement
x^{i_{k}}
of a connected component and they are arranged in the matching order with
x^{i_{1}}
being the enhancement of the component containing
v_{d}
.

Let
T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n})}\in{{CST_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
and
e\in\operatorname{IA}(T)
, then suppose
(i_{k},i_{k+1})
be the pair such that the components containing both the vertices incident to
e
have enhancements
x^{i_{k}}
and
x^{i_{k+1}}
. We call
e
to be
non-vanishing
if
(i_{k}+i_{k+1})<m
.

Let
\widetilde{T}
denote the tree obtained by contracting internally inactive edges of
T
. Suppose
P(e)
be a path in
\widetilde{T}
beginning with
e
with
e
being non-vanishing and
V(P(e))=\{v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{k}\}
, then denote
H_{\epsilon}^{P(e)}
to be the enhanced spanning subgraph where
H_{\epsilon}^{P(e)}=H_{c}^{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
as spanning subgraphs and define
\epsilon
as

\epsilon(v)=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{c}(v),&\text{if }v\not\in P\\
x^{i_{k}+i_{k+1}},&\text{if }v=v_{1}\\
\epsilon_{c}(v_{j+1}),&\text{if }v=v_{j},\ 2\leq j\leq n-1\\
1,&\text{if }v=v_{k}\end{cases}

where,
e=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
with
v_{1}
being closer to
v_{d}
than
v_{2}
.

Let
P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k}):=P(e_{1})\cup P(e_{2})\cup\cdots\cup P(e_{k})
where each
P(e_{i})
is a path in
\widetilde{T}
such that
E(P(e_{i}))\cap E(P(e_{j}))=\phi
for all
i\neq j
and each
P(e_{i})
begins with
e_{i}
, where each
e_{i}
is non-vanishing. Denote
H_{(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})}^{P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k})}
to be the enhanced spanning subgraph where,
H_{(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})}^{T(f_{1},\cdots,f_{k})}=H_{c}^{{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}}
as spanning subgraphs and

(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})(v):=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{c}(v),&\text{if }v\not\in P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k})\\
\epsilon_{i}(v),&\text{if }v\in P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k})\end{cases}

where, each
\epsilon_{i}
is the enhancement of
H^{P(e_{i})}
.

The fact coming from Proposition
3.14
also holds in this scenario too. We now characterize all the enhanced spanning subraphs
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
for which there is an alternating path from
H_{c}^{{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}}
to
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
in
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{c}^{{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}},H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}})=1
if and only if
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}=H_{(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})}^{P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k})}
as enhanced spanning subgraphs for some
P(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{k})
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to Lemma
3.15
. We just need to arrange each
P(e_{i})
in the matching order of
[\mathcal{R}(T),T]
coming from Theorem
4.1
. See Figure
21
.
∎

We now define an explicit form of the differential
\partial_{ST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}
in the spanning tree complex
{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
T
be a NBC spanning tree and
e\in\operatorname{EN}(T)
with
e=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
. Let
P_{v_{i}}
be the unique path from
v_{d}
to
v_{i}
in
\widetilde{T}
for
i=1,2
and
\operatorname{NV}(P_{v_{i}})
be the collection of non-vanishing internally active edges in
P_{v_{i}}
. Then,

(7)

\displaystyle\partial_{ST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}\left(T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\operatorname{EN}(T)\\
\psi_{e}(T)\in\operatorname{NBC}(G)\\
i(\psi_{e}(T))=i(T)+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\xi(T,e)}

\displaystyle\bigg((-1)^{\xi(T,e)}.\psi_{e}(T)^{(i_{1},\cdots,\epsilon(C_{v_{1}}).\epsilon(C_{v_{2}}),\cdots,i_{n+1})}\ +

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{f\in\operatorname{NV}(P_{v_{i}})}(-1)^{L_{i}(f,e)+\xi(T,e)}.\psi_{e}(T)^{(j_{1},\cdots,j_{n})}\bigg)

where,
L_{i}(f,e):=\#\{g\mid g\in\operatorname{IA}(T),\ g\text{ lies between }f\text{ and }e\text{ with }g\in P_{v_{i}}\}
and

j_{k}=\begin{cases}i_{k},&\text{ if }C\subset P\neq P_{v_{i}}\\
\epsilon(H^{P_{v_{i}}}),&\text{ if }C\subset P_{v_{i}}\\
\epsilon(C_{v_{j}}),&\text{ if }e\in C_{v_{j}}\text{ and }j\neq i\end{cases}

Proof.

By the virtue of Lemma
3.12
, it is enough to describe all the alternating paths between
T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}
and
\psi_{e}(T)^{(j_{1},\cdots,j_{n})}
where
e\in\operatorname{EN}(T)
. Now suppose
\widetilde{v_{1}}
and
\widetilde{v_{2}}
be the two end vertices of
e
in
\widetilde{T}
together with
\epsilon(\widetilde{v_{1}})=x^{i_{k}}
and
\epsilon(\widetilde{v_{2}})=x^{i_{k}^{\prime}}
. Then either
(i_{k}+i_{k}^{\prime})<m
or
(i_{k}+i_{k}^{\prime})=m
. For the former case, we will have an alternating path of length
1
from
T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}
to
\psi_{e}(T)^{(i_{1},\cdots,\epsilon(C_{v_{1}}).\epsilon(C_{v_{2}}),\cdots,i_{n+1})}
which is obtained by adding the edge
e
to
H_{c}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
which provides us the initial summand in the equation
7
. The weight of this path is
(-1)^{\xi(T,e)}
. This summand can also be zero if the latter case holds.

Now in general, for any alternating path
\mathcal{P}
we have a choice of an enhanced spanning subgraph
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
for which
\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(H_{c}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}},H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}})=1
, which is characterized by Lemma
4.3
. Now since any
\mathcal{P}
preserves the
j-
grading of the enhanced spanning subgraphs in
\mathcal{P}
, so we consider only such
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
for which
H_{\epsilon}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}=H^{P_{v_{i}}(f)}_{\epsilon}
, where
f\in\operatorname{NV}(P_{v_{i}})
and the end vertex of
P_{v_{i}}(f)
is either of
\widetilde{v_{i}}
for
i=1,2
. Thus, such an alternating path
\mathcal{P}
starts from
H_{c}^{T^{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n+1})}}
, reaches to
H^{P_{v_{i}}(f)}_{\epsilon}
where either
\epsilon(\widetilde{v_{1}})=1
or
\epsilon(\widetilde{v_{2}})=1
and then we add the edge
e
to obtain
d\left(H^{P_{v_{i}}(f)}_{\epsilon}\right)
whose enhancement are same as
H^{P_{v_{i}}(f)}_{\epsilon}
on all the components except the component containing
e
, which has the enhancement
\epsilon(\widetilde{v_{j}})
with
j\neq i
. The weight of
\mathcal{P}
in this case is given by

w(\mathcal{P})=(-1)^{L_{i}(f,e)+\xi(T,e)}

∎

4.1.
Homological Span of Chromatic Homology

In this subsection, we provide a proof of a conjecture posed by Sazdanovic and Scofield
[
SS18
]
.

Theorem 4.5

(Conjecture 43)
.

[
SS18
]

The homological span of chromatic homology over algebra
\mathcal{A}_{m}
of any graph
G
with
v
vertices and
b
blocks is equal to
hspan\left({{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}\right)=v-b
.

It should be noted that they proved this result for the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
algebra and conjectured that it should hold for any
\mathcal{A}_{m}
algebra based on computational evidence. In their paper, they also showed that as the girth of the graph approaches infinity, the span of Khovanov homology tends to infinity. We reprove this result using the spanning tree model.

Before proving the result, we first recall some basic definitions.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
G
be a graph. Then a block
B
is a of
G
is either a maximal
2
-connected subgraph of
G
or a bridge.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
i_{min}
and
i_{max}
are the minimum and maximum homological grading of chromatic homology non trivial homology groups for a graph
G
.
hspan\left({{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}\right):=i_{max}-i_{min}+1
.

Proof of Theorem

1.3
.

It is well known that
i_{min}=0
. It also follows from the fact that if
T_{1}
is the minimum NBC spanning tree with respect to the lexicographic order then
[{T_{1}^{x^{m-1},x^{m},\cdots,x^{m}}}]\in{{HST_{0,mv-1}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
is a non-zero homology class.

We will show that in a graph with
b
blocks, there exists a a unique NBC spanning tree
T_{max}
that has maximum homological grading and has exactly
b
live edges. As a consequence, the homological degree of
T_{max}
is
v-b-1
. Now, in the spanning tree complex
{{CST_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
,
[{T_{max}}^{1,x,\cdots,x}]
(i.e., the enhanced spanning tree with enhancement
1
in the component containing
v_{d}
and enhancement
x
in other components) is obviously a cycle. Furthermore, by Theorem
4.4
,
[{T_{max}}^{1,x,\cdots,x}]\in{{HST_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}
is a non-zero homology class. This will prove that
hspan\left({{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}\right)=v-b
.

(1)

Let
B
be a block that is
2
-connected. Recall that for a two-connected graph, there exists an
ear decomposition
. An ear decomposition of
B
is a nested sequence
(G_{0},G_{1},\cdots,G_{k})
of non-separable subgraphs such that

(a)

G_{0}
is a cycle,

(b)

G_{i+1}=G_{i}\cup P_{i}
, where
P_{i}
is an ear of
G_{i}
in
G
for all
0\leq i<k
,

(c)

G_{k}=B
.

We fix an ordering of edges in
B
such that whenever
i<j
, we have
e<f\ \forall e\in G_{i}
and
j\in G_{j}
.

Now, we construct the tree
T_{B}
by removing the second smallest edge from
G_{0}
and the smallest edge from each ear. It is easy to see that
T_{B}
is a NBC spanning tree. Furthermore, the only live edge in
T_{B}
is the smallest edge in
G_{0}
.

(2)

If
B
is a block that is a bridge, then in any spanning tree of
G
,
B
will be a live edge. In fact, we define
T_{B}=B
in this case.

(3)

Given a block decomposition
(B_{1},\cdots,B_{b})
of a connected graph
G
,

T_{max}:=T_{B_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup T_{B_{b}}.

It is clear that
T_{max}
is an NBC spanning tree with
b
live edges. It is also clear that in any NBC spanning tree there should be at least one
1
live edge as otherwise it would contain a broken circuit. Finally, the uniqueness of
T_{max}
follows from the fact that any basis exchange (i.e. removing an internal edge
e
and add an external edge
f
in
T_{max}
decreases the homological grading. This concludes our construction.

∎

Now we will establish a more general theorem about the effect on chromatic homology for operation of vertex gluing.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
G
be a connected graph obtained by gluing two connected graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
in a single vertex. Then,
{{HST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G)\cong{{HST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{1})\otimes{{HST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{2})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the root is placed at the vertex where the two graphs are glued. This vertex will also serve as the root when considering the graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
individually.

Suppose
T
is a spanning tree of
G
. Then,
T_{i}=T\cap G_{i}
is a spanning tree of
G_{i}
. Since
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
share the root vertex, for any critical state
(\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon)
of
G
, the enhancement
\epsilon
decomposes as
\epsilon_{1}\otimes\epsilon_{2}
, where
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
are enhancements of
\operatorname{IN}(T_{1})
and
\operatorname{IN}(T_{2})
, respectively.

Consider any two critical states
(\operatorname{IN}(T_{i}),\epsilon_{i})
and
(\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime}_{i}),\epsilon^{\prime}_{i})
of
G_{i}
. Let
T^{\prime}
be a spanning tree of
G
such that
T^{\prime}_{i}=T^{\prime}\cap G_{i}
. Let
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
be any two enhancements of
\operatorname{IN}(T)
in
G
such that they restrict to the enhancements
\epsilon_{i}
of
\operatorname{IN}(T_{i})
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
of
\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime}_{i})
, respectively, in the chromatic complex of
G_{i}
. Since
\operatorname{cyc}(T_{i},e)=\operatorname{cyc}(T,e)
for any external edge
e
, using the description of
\partial_{ST}
in Theorem
4.4
, it follows that

[\partial_{ST}^{G_{i}}\left((\operatorname{IN}(T_{i}),\epsilon_{i})\right):(\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime}_{i}),\epsilon^{\prime}_{i})]=[\partial_{ST}^{G}\left((\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon)\right):(\operatorname{IN}(T^{\prime}),\epsilon^{\prime})].

We define the following chain map:

\Psi:{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G)\to{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{1})\otimes{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{2}),

\Psi(\operatorname{IN}(T),\epsilon):=(\operatorname{IN}(T_{1}),\epsilon_{1})\otimes(\operatorname{IN}(T_{2}),\epsilon_{2}).

Similarly, given two spanning trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, respectively, one can construct a spanning tree
T=T_{1}\cup T_{2}
of
G
. We define the map:
Similarly, given two spanning trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, respectively, one can construct a spanning tree
T=T_{1}\cup T_{2}
of
G
. We define the map:

\Upsilon:{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{1})\otimes{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G_{2})\to{{CST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G),

\Upsilon((\operatorname{IN}(T_{1}),\epsilon_{1})\otimes(\operatorname{IN}(T_{2}),\epsilon_{2}))=(\operatorname{IN}(T_{1}\cup T_{2}),\epsilon_{1}\otimes\epsilon_{2}).

It is easy to see that
\Upsilon=\Psi^{-1}
, and it is also a chain map. Hence,
\Psi
is a quasi-isomorphism.∎

Remark 4.9
.

It should be noted that the above quasi-isomorphism doesn’t preserve the quantum grading.

4.2.
Existence of
m
torsion in
{{H_{*,*}}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)}

Theorem 4.10
.

The cohomology
{H_{\ast}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(G)
of a graph
G
contains a torsion part if and only if
G
has no loops and contains a cycle of order greater than or equal to
3
. In this case,
H_{\ast}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}(G)
has a torsion of order dividing
m
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the theorem for connected graphs. First, assume that
G
is a
2
-connected graph. Let
T_{M}
be the unique NBC spanning tree in the maximum homological grading. By Theorem
4.5
,
T_{M}
has exactly one live edge. Let
T_{M-1}
be the NBC spanning tree preceding
T_{max}
in the lexicographical order with
|T_{M-1}\cap T_{M}|=|T_{M}|-1
i.e.
T_{M}=T_{M-1}\setminus\{e\}\cup\{f\}
. Also since there is a unique NBC tree the maximum homological grading ( The unique NBC tree with exactly one live edge
e_{m}
), it follows that
i(T_{M})-i(T_{M-1})=1
,
\operatorname{IN}(T_{M})\setminus\operatorname{IN}(T_{M-1})=\{f\}
and
\operatorname{IA}(T_{M-1})\setminus\operatorname{IA}(T_{M})=\{e\}
.

Now, consider the following chain element:

S=\left(\sum_{i+j=m-1}T_{M-1}^{x,x^{i},x^{j}}-\sum_{i+j=m}T_{M-1}^{1,x^{i},x^{j}}\right)

We use diagrammatic notations in order to denote the enhanced spanning subgraphs of
[\mathcal{R}(T_{M-1}),T_{M-1}]
and
[\mathcal{R}(T_{M}),T_{M}]
. Then
\partial_{ST}(S)
is given by the following expression: (Also see Figure
22
for all possible alternating paths to
T_{M}^{x,x^{m-1}}
)

Out of these four possibilities, one can easily show that (b) and (d) will vanish under

since in both the cases there is only one internally active edge in the cycle space of the externally inactive edge. Thus, if

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In the above calculation,

a_{i}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T_{M})\\
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{M})\end{subarray}}a_{T,i},\ b_{i}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T_{M})\\
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{M})\end{subarray}}b_{T,i},\ c_{i}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T_{M})\\
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{M})\end{subarray}}c_{T,i}\text{ and }d_{i}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T_{M})\\
e\in\operatorname{IN}(T_{M})\end{subarray}}d_{T,i}.

Comparing the coefficients, we have the following identities:

(8)

\displaystyle-(a_{1}+a_{m-1})+(c_{1}+c_{m-1})+\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}=1

(9)

\displaystyle-(a_{i}+a_{m-i})-(b_{i-1}+b_{m-i})+(c_{i}+c_{m-i})+(d_{i-1}+d_{m-i})=0,\qquad 2\leq i\leq m-1

(10)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}d_{i}=0

For
i=m-1
, combining (7) and (8) we get,

(11)

-(b_{m-2}+b_{1})+(d_{m-2}+d_{1})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)-1

Similarly for
i=2
, combining (8) and (10) we get,

(12)

-(a_{2}+a_{m-2})+(c_{2}+c_{m-2})=1-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)

Suppose
m
is odd and of the form
m=2k+1
then continuing in the above manner we arrive at
i=k+1
in order to get,

(13)

-2b_{k}+2d_{k}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)-1

Now we multiply all the equations with
2
except for (12) and add them all to get,

-2\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}+2\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}d_{i}=(m-2)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}-1\right)

Now using (9) we have,
m\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)=(m-2)
which implies
m\mid m-2
which is not possible since,
m
is odd. Thus, we have a contradiction.

When
m
is even of the form
2k
then instead of equation (12) we will have

-(b_{k-1}+b_{k})+(d_{k-1}+d_{k})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)-1

So, adding all the equations on
b_{i}
and
d_{i}
from
i=2
to
i=k+1
, we have

-\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}d_{i}=\frac{m}{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}-1\right)

Again using (9) we get,
(m+2)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}b_{i}\right)=m
which implies
m+2\mid m
which is a contradiction.

Hence, considering upto signs of both
T_{M}^{x,x^{m-1}}
and
(-1)^{\xi(\psi^{\prime}_{e}(T_{M}),e)}
we conclude that
T_{M}^{x,x^{m-1}}
is not exact. Thus, we have a torsion of order dividing
m
in
{{HST}^{\mathcal{A}_{m}}}(B)
. Now, the general case follows from Theorem
4.8
.
∎
